Dependable Exploitation of High-Dimensional Unlabeled Data in an Assumption-Lean Framework
Chao Ying, Siyi Deng, Yang Ning, Jiwei Zhao, Heping Zhang

Abstract
Semi-supervised learning has attracted significant attention due to the proliferation of applications featuring limited labeled data but abundant unlabeled data. In this paper, we examine the statistical inference problem in an assumption-lean framework which involves a high-dimensional regression parameter, defined by minimizing the least squares, within the context of semi-supervised learning. We investigate when and how unlabeled data can enhance the estimation efficiency of a regression parameter functional. First, we demonstrate that a straightforward debiased estimator can only be more efficient than its supervised counterpart if the unknown conditional mean function can be consistently estimated at an appropriate rate. Otherwise, incorporating unlabeled data can actually be counterproductive. To address this vulnerability, we propose a novel estimator guaranteed to be at least as efficient as the supervised baseline, even when the conditional mean function is misspecified. This ensures the dependable use of unlabeled data for statistical inference. Finally, we extend our approach to the general M-estimation framework, and demonstrate the effectiveness of our methodology through comprehensive simulation studies and a real data application.



Key Words:

Semi-supervised learning, model misspecification, high-dimensional inference, assumption-lean framework, efficiency gain, semiparametric efficiency.

1
Introduction

1.1
Motivation: abundant high-dimensional unlabeled data

Unlabeled data are typically more abundant than labeled data, as collecting raw information is often far easier and less costly than manual annotation.
In many real-world settings, data are continuously generated through sensors, transactions, medical records, and user interactions, while labeling requires human expertise, time, and resources.
For example, in healthcare, vast amounts of electronic health records (EHRs) are generated daily, but annotating them for specific diseases or conditions requires expert clinicians.
Similarly, in computer vision, millions of images and videos are uploaded online, yet labeling them with precise object categories demands significant manual effort.

Unlabeled data are often high-dimensional because they are collected from complex real-world sources that capture a vast array of features without prior filtering or annotation.
For instance, in EHRs, a single patient’s history can include numerous lab results, diagnoses, prescriptions, and physiological measurements, each contributing to a high-dimensional feature space.
In computer vision, images and videos consist of millions of pixels, each representing a separate dimension.
Likewise, in natural language processing, raw text data encompass extensive vocabulary and contextual dependencies, making them inherently high-dimensional.

1.2
Our approach: dependable exploitation in an assumption-lean framework

Throughout, suppose we observe
n
independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) samples
({\bf X}_{1},Y_{1}),\cdots,({\bf X}_{n},Y_{n})\sim({\bf X},Y)
from the labeled data, and
N
i.i.d. samples,
{\bf X}_{n+1},\cdots,

{\bf X}_{N+n}\sim{\bf X}
, from the unlabeled data.
For notation simplicity, we denote by
\mathbb{X}=({\bf X}_{1},\cdots,{\bf X}_{n})^{\rm T}

\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
,
{\bf Y}=(Y_{1},\cdots,Y_{n})^{\rm T}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, and
\widetilde{\mathbb{X}}=({\bf X}_{1},\cdots,{\bf X}_{N+n})^{\rm T}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{(N+n)\times p}
.
Note that
p
can be much larger than
n
, and there is no requirement between
N
and
n
.

Due to the complex and heterogeneous nature of abundant unlabeled data, estimation problems can be casted in the general
assumption-lean framework
(
8
;
5
)
, where the parameter of interest
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
is defined via minimizing a loss function such as the least squares, while the true conditional mean might be hard or even infeasible to be consistently estimated under high-dimensionality.
To be more specific, we consider

Y=f({\bf X})+\epsilon,

(1)

where
f({\bf X})
is the conditional mean function
{\hbox{E}}(Y|{\bf X})
,
\epsilon
is independent of
{\bf X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
with
{\hbox{E}}(\epsilon)=0
,
{\hbox{E}}(\epsilon^{2})=\sigma^{2}
, and
\sigma^{2}
is an unknown parameter.
For simplicity, we assume
{\hbox{E}}({\bf X})={\bf 0}
and
{\hbox{E}}\{f({\bf X})\}=0
.
Additionally, we consider linear regression as the working model which leads to the intercept term as
0
.
Since
{\hbox{E}}\{(Y-{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}})^{2}\}={\hbox{E}}[\{f({\bf X})-{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}\}^{2}]+\sigma^{2}
, the regression coefficients in a linear model correspond to the
L_{2}({\mathbb{P}})
projection of
f({\bf X})
onto the linear space spanned by
{\bf X}
, i.e.,

{\bm{\theta}}^{*}=\arg\min_{{\bm{\theta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}{\hbox{E}}[\{f({\bf X})-{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}\}^{2}]\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
,
which describes the linear dependence between
Y
and
{\bf X}
.
Our goal is to construct an asymptotically normal estimator of the linear functional of
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
that, no matter whether the linear model is correctly specified or
f({\bf X})
is consistently estimated, is guaranteed to be no less efficient than the supervised estimator (the one that uses labeled data only; e.g., debiased lasso estimator).

As noted above, the parameter
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
admits a clear interpretation under misspecification of model (
1
). The proposed method naturally extends to more sophisticated settings such as the M-estimation; we defer details to Section
4
.

1.3
Our novel contributions

Adopting the assumption-lean framework,

18
proposed estimators of
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
that can have a faster convergence rate than the supervised estimators and can achieve the optimal rate under certain conditions, but they did not study the asymptotic variance of the proposed estimator.
Indeed, the limiting distribution of their proposed estimators are intractable due to the regularization.

In this paper, distinct from
18
,
our ultimate goal is to answer the question:

How to propose an estimator of the linear functional of
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
, that is asymptotically normal, and is guaranteed to be no less efficient than the supervised estimator, no matter whether the linear model is correctly specified or the conditional mean function is consistently estimated?

To answer this question, a natural choice is the debiased estimator; e.g., one can construct a one-step debiased estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
, using
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{SD}
proposed in
18
as the initial (details are given in Section
2.2
).
Indeed, one can show that the corresponding estimator
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
, with any
{\bm{v}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
, is asymptotically normal and can attain the semiparametric efficiency bound under certain conditions.
However, its performance depends on the consistency of the estimate of
f({\bf X})
.
If
f({\bf X})
cannot be consistently estimated,
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
is not guaranteed to be more efficient than the supervised debiased lasso estimator.
Therefore, this debiased approach does not answer the question above well.

In this paper,
we propose a dependable semi-supervised estimator which does not require the estimate of
f({\bf X})
. The main idea is to construct a set of unbiased estimating functions and decorrelate the score function to reduce the variability.
In Theorem
1
below, we show that the proposed estimator
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{S,\psi}^{d}
is asymptotically normal, where
\psi
is a tuning parameter.
When the linear model is misspecified and
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{n}{n+N}=\rho
for some
0\leq\rho<1
, the estimator
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{S,\psi}^{d}
with
0<\psi<2
is strictly more efficient than the debiased lasso, leading to more powerful hypothesis tests and shorter confidence intervals.
We attain the maximum variance reduction by choosing
\psi=1
.
In addition, if either the linear model is correctly specified
f({\bf X})={\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
or the size of the unlabeled data is small in that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{n}{n+N}=1
(i.e.,
N\ll n
), the estimator
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{S,\psi}^{d}
is asymptotically equivalent to the debiased lasso estimator.
In summary, the estimator
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{S,\psi}^{d}
provides a dependable use of the unlabeled data, since it is always no worse than the supervised estimators, no matter whether the linear model is correctly specified or the conditional mean function is consistently estimated.

1.4
Relevant literature

The benefits of using abundant unlabeled data have been popularly investigated by both computer scientists and statisticians.
In problems with discrete labels, researchers have proposed a variety of classification algorithms under common assumptions such as manifold assumption and cluster assumption; see, e.g.,
39
;
52
and comprehensive survey articles
(
64
;
15
)
.
In nonparametric regression,
53
developed an estimator that can improve the rate of the mean squared error under the semi-supervised smoothness assumption; also see
28
for an extensive review of semi-supervised regression.

In recent years, significant progress has been made on utilizing unlabeled data for parameter estimation or empirical risk minimization
(
59
)
. However, the question we posed in Section
1.3
, along with the goal we aim to achieve in this paper, has not been thoroughly addressed in the literature.
The relevant studies reviewed in this section either impose restrictive conditions or yield limited results.

In the low and fixed dimensionality setting, for parameter estimation,
13
and
3
proposed estimators that are more efficient than the least square estimator that uses labeled data only, for each component of
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
.
However, the efficiency improvement has no guarantee for the linear combination of
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
such as
\theta^{*}_{1}+\theta^{*}_{2}
.

Allowing the dimensionality to grow with
n
, the sample size of the labeled data,
60
proposed a general semi-supervised inference framework to improve the estimation of the population mean
{\hbox{E}}(Y)
without specific distributional assumptions relating the outcome
Y
and the covariate
{\bf X}
.
However,
60
can only allow the dimensionality to grow with a no faster than
n^{1/2}
rate.
Similar conclusions could also be found under the general M-estimation framework
(
43
)
.

With high-dimensional data,
62
proposed semi-supervised estimators of population mean and variance and established their asymptotic distributions; however, they required
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{n}{n+N}<1/2
to guarantee the dependable inference on
{\hbox{E}}(Y)
(i.e., more efficient than the sample mean of
Y
in the labeled data).
In the high dimensional regime,
10
considered how to estimate the explained variance
{{\bm{\theta}}^{*}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Sigma}}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
in the semi-supervised setting.
Their estimator achieved the optimal rate of convergence and was asymptotically normal; however, their results were established under the assumption that the working linear model is correctly specified, which differed from the assumption-lean framework.
In a high dimensional linear regression setting, the parameter of interest studied in
16
is the regression coefficient associated with one particular covariate, and this particular covariate plays the critical role in defining the discrepancy between the distributions of the labeled and unlabeled data.

16
studied the benefits of the unlabeled data in terms of enhancing efficiency and robustness, but their proposed methods heavily rely on the model among the covariates under different sparse or dense structures.
Additionally,
14
concentrated on quantile estimation under high dimensionality, adopting the similar idea as
43
.
In the absence of sparsity,
29
studied the problem of estimating the conditional variance of
{\bf X}
given
Y
in a linear regression model.
While
22
proposed a notable approach termed surrogate assisted semi-supervised inference, their estimator remained vulnerable because its consistency depended entirely on a correctly specified imputation model.
Also, their framework lacked guarantees for the dependable use of unlabeled data.

Our work is related to a growing literature that strengthens statistical inference by leveraging AI/ML predictions
(
51
;
32
)
.
For example, prediction-powered inference (PPI), introduced by
1
, is a semi-supervised framework that uses predictions from an AI/ML model to enable valid statistical inference; however, PPI can be less efficient than the naive labeled-only estimator so may fail to reliably leverage unlabeled data.
In response, several methods were proposed over the past three years to address this efficiency gap.
For scalar parameters,
2
proposed a simple tuning procedure that guarantees efficiency gain even under prediction-model misspecification.
More broadly,
31
developed a post-prediction adaptive inference approach that guarantees valid inference without assumptions on the quality of the ML predictions;
20
studied inference under squared-error loss; and
42
extended these ideas to settings with multiple sets of predictions.
Despite these advances, this line of work typically defines the target parameter in a standard low-dimensional setting.
In contrast, in this paper, we study how to reliably exploit unlabeled data in an assumption-lean, high-dimensional regime, where the parameter of interest is defined through the high-dimensional coefficient vector in a linear working model.
This setting is particularly relevant when the goal is to characterize the association between the response and a high-dimensional covariate (or a functional thereof).
To attain efficiency dominance, our proposed estimator does not require estimating
f({\bf X})
; it is therefore prediction-free and agnostic to the outcome mean model.
In addition, accounting for high dimensionality is central to our theoretical analysis and introduces additional technical challenges.

1.5
Structure of the paper and notation

Structure

In what follows, Section
2
provides preliminary materials, with Section
2.2
reviewing the straightforward debiased estimator which relies on a consistent estimate of
f({\bf X})
.
In Section
3
, we propose a novel dependable procedure which does not rely on the estimation of
f({\bf X})
and is guaranteed to be no worse than the one using the labeled data only.
Our proposal can be naturally extended to more sophisticated settings such as the M-estimation, with details in Section
4
.
Numerical experiments and a real data application are in Sections
5
and
6
, respectively.
The paper is concluded with a discussion in Section
7
.
All the technical proofs are contained in the Supplement.

Notation

For
{\bm{v}}=(v^{(1)},\cdots,v^{(p)})^{\rm T}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, and
1\leq q\leq\infty
, we define
\norm{\bv}_{q}=(\sum_{i=1}^{p}|v^{(i)}|^{q})^{1/q}
,
\norm{\bv}_{0}=|\{i:v^{(i)}\neq 0\}|
, where
|A|
is the cardinality of a set
A
. Denote
\norm{\bv}_{\infty}=\max_{1\leq i\leq p}|v^{(i)}|
and
{\bm{v}}^{\otimes 2}={\bm{v}}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}
.
For a matrix
\mathbb{M}=[\mathbb{M}_{ij}]
,
define
\norm{\bM}_{\max}=\max_{ij}|\mathbb{M}_{ij}|
,
\norm{\bM}_{1}=\max_{j}\sum_{i}|\mathbb{M}_{ij}|
,
\norm{\bM}_{\infty}=\max_{i}\sum_{j}|\mathbb{M}_{ij}|
.
For
S\subseteq\{1,\cdots,p\}
, let
{\bm{v}}_{S}=\{v^{(k)}:k\in S\}
and
S^{c}
be the complement of
S
.
For matrix
\mathbb{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
and index set
L\subseteq\{1,\cdots,n\}
,
\mathbb{X}_{L}=\{\mathbb{X}_{i\cdot}:i\in L\}^{\rm T}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|L|\times p}
.
For two positive sequences
a_{n}
and
b_{n}
, we write
a_{n}\asymp b_{n}
if
C\leq a_{n}/b_{n}\leq C^{\prime}
for some
C,C^{\prime}>0
. Similarly, we use
a\lesssim b
to denote
a\leq Cb
for some constant
C>0
.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Review of estimation and inference with labeled data only

In supervised learning that only uses the labeled data, there are a large number of penalized methods for estimating
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
, such as lasso
(
44
)
and Dantzig selector
(
12
)
.
The supervised Dantzig selector is defined as

\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{D}=\arg\min\|{\bm{\theta}}\|_{1},~~\textrm{s.t.}~~\Big\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_{i}-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}){\bf X}_{i}\Big\|_{\infty}\leq\lambda_{D},

(2)

where
\lambda_{D}
is a tuning parameter.
Similarly, the supervised lasso estimator is defined as

\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{L}=\argmin_{{\bm{\theta}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_{i}-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}})^{2}/(2n)+\lambda_{L}\norm{\bt}_{1}
.
The Dantzig selector and lasso are theoretically equivalent
(
45
)
.
For statistical inference, there has been some recent research on debiased lasso estimators for hypothesis tests and confidence intervals, for example,
61
;
47
;
24
;
9
;
34
;
33
, a list that is far from exhaustive.
Under certain regularity conditions such as
s\log p/\sqrt{n}=o(1)
, following the proof in
7
, one can show that, the debiased lasso estimator for
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
is
\sqrt{n}
-consistent and the corresponding asymptotic variance equals
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}\mathbb{K}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}
, where
{\bm{\Omega}}={\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1}
is the precision matrix and

\mathbb{K}={\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i1}^{\otimes 2})
; see Remark
2
in Section
3.3
.

While considerable progress has been made towards understanding estimation and inference in the fully supervised setting, research in the semi-supervised setting remains limited.
Notably, under model (
1
),
where linear regression serves as the working model, the covariate
{\bf X}
no longer functions as an ancillary statistic for the regression parameter
\bm{\theta}^{*}
. Therefore, the information of
{\bf X}
in the unlabeled data may improve the estimation and inference of
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
.

2.2
Straightforward debiased estimator

In an earlier work,
18
proposed a semi-supervised estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{SD}
in their Section 3.
As long as the conditional mean function is consistently estimated by
\widehat{f}(\cdot)
and some regularity conditions are satisfied, Theorem 3.2 in
18
showed that
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{SD}
is minimax optimal.
To be more specific,

\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{SD}=\arg\min\|{\bm{\theta}}\|_{1},~~\textrm{s.t.}~~\|\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{n+N}{\bm{\theta}}-\widehat{\bm{\xi}}\|_{\infty}\leq\lambda_{SD},

(3)

where the cross-fitting technique (with details provided in Algorithm
1
) is used to compute
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}
; i.e.,

\widehat{\bm{\xi}}=(\widehat{\bm{\xi}}_{1}+\widehat{\bm{\xi}}_{2})/2
,

\widehat{\bm{\xi}}_{j}=\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}{\bf X}_{i}Y_{i}-\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}{\bf X}_{i}\widehat{f}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})+\frac{1}{n_{j}+N_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}}{\bf X}_{i}\widehat{f}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})
.

Motivated by the formulation of the regularized estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{SD}
in (
3
), one can view
h(\widetilde{\mathbb{X}},{\bf Y};{\bm{\theta}})=\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{n+N}{\bm{\theta}}-\widehat{\bm{\xi}}
as an estimating function for
{\bm{\theta}}
.
Borrowing the idea from the classical one-step estimator and the debiased lasso, one can easily construct

\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}=\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{SD}-\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}h(\widetilde{\mathbb{X}},{\bf Y};\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{SD})=\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{SD}+\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}(\widehat{\bm{\xi}}-\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{n+N}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{SD}),

(4)

where
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}
is an estimator of
{\bm{\Omega}}={\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1}

with details provided in Supplement
S.3.2
.

In Supplement
S.3.1
, we detail the theoretical properties of the debiased estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
.
More specifically, under some assumptions and certain regularity conditions, the debiased estimator
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
is
\sqrt{n}
-consistent, and the corresponding asymptotic variance is
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\sigma^{2}{\bm{\Omega}}+\frac{n}{n+N}{\bm{\Gamma}}){\bm{v}}
, where
{\bm{\Gamma}}={\hbox{E}}[{\bf W}^{\otimes 2}\{f({\bf X})-{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\}^{2}]
and
{\bf W}={\bm{\Omega}}{\bf X}
.
Moreover, one can show that

n^{1/2}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})/\{{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\sigma}^{2}\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}+\frac{n}{n+N}\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}){\bm{v}}\}^{1/2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
,
where the specific form of the estimators
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}
,
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}
are provided in Supplement
S.3.2
.

However, the debiased estimator has a serious drawback.
The consistency and asymptotic normality of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
relies on the consistent estimation of the conditional mean function
f({\bf X})
, say,
\widehat{f}({\bf X})
.
If, unfortunately,
f({\bf X})
cannot be consistently estimated, the estimator
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
is not guaranteed to be more efficient than the supervised debiased lasso estimators
(
47
)
.
This is not an ideal phenomenon.
The main goal of this paper, with the novel method presented below in Section
3
, is to propose an estimator that is guaranteed to be always more efficient than the supervised debiased lasso estimators, thus provides the dependable use of the unlabeled data.

3
Proposed Estimator towards Dependable

Semi-Supervised Inference

To exploit high-dimensional unlabeled data, the most difficult step is to estimate
the conditional mean function
f({\bf X})
correctly.
In this section we propose a novel dependable semi-supervised inference approach, which does not rely on the estimation of the conditional mean function but guarantees the efficiency gain compared to the supervised approach, thus provides the dependable use of the unlabeled data.

3.1
Motivation

Given any
p
-dimensional function
m({\bf X})\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{p}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, define
{\bm{\mu}}={\hbox{E}}\{{\bf X}m({\bf X})\}
, then
{\bf X}m({\bf X})-{\bm{\mu}}
is an unbiased estimating function for zero.
While it does not directly involve the unknown parameter
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
, it does play an important role in the dependable semi-supervised inference approach.
Using
{\bf X}m({\bf X})-{\bm{\mu}}
as the covariate, we postulate a
p
-variate working regression model with response variable
{\bf X}(Y-{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})
; i.e.,

{\bf X}(Y-{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})=\mathbb{B}^{\rm T}\{{\bf X}m({\bf X})-{\bm{\mu}}\}+{\bf E},

(5)

where
{\bf E}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
is the error vector and the coefficient matrix
\mathbb{B}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times p}
is

\mathbb{B}=({\hbox{E}}[\{{\bf X}m({\bf X})-{\bm{\mu}}\}^{\otimes 2}])^{-1}{\hbox{E}}\{{\bf X}^{\otimes 2}m({\bf X})(Y-{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})\}
.
Since (
5
) is only a working model, the error
{\bf E}
and the covariate
{\bf X}m({\bf X})-{\bm{\mu}}
are not necessarily independent. Recall that the response variable
{\bf X}(Y-{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})
corresponds to the score function of
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
in the linear regression model, and can be rewritten as
{\bf X}\{\epsilon+\eta({\bf X})\}
, where
\epsilon=Y-f({\bf X})
and
\eta({\bf X})=f({\bf X})-{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
is the nonlinear effect. Since
\epsilon
and
{\bf X}m({\bf X})-{\bm{\mu}}
are independent, the goal of model (
5
) is to explain the nonlinear effect
{\bf X}\eta({\bf X})
by the covariate
{\bf X}m({\bf X})-{\bm{\mu}}
. Indeed, we show in Remark
3
that the optimal choice of
m({\bf X})
is
\eta({\bf X})
and in this case the nonlinear effect
{\bf X}\eta({\bf X})
can be perfectly explained by
{\bf X}m({\bf X})-{\bm{\mu}}
.

Given
{\bf X}m({\bf X})-{\bm{\mu}}
and the coefficient matrix
\mathbb{B}
, we define a class of unbiased estimating functions for
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
as
h_{\psi}({\bf X},Y;{\bm{\theta}})={\bf X}(Y-{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}})-\psi\mathbb{B}^{\rm T}\{{\bf X}m({\bf X})-{\bm{\mu}}\}=\bar{\bm{\xi}}_{\psi}-{\bf X}{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}
, where

\bar{\bm{\xi}}_{\psi}={\bf X}Y-\psi\mathbb{B}^{\rm T}\{{\bf X}m({\bf X})-{\bm{\mu}}\},

(6)

with
\psi\in{\mathbb{R}}
being a tuning parameter that balances two unbiased functions
{\bf X}m({\bf X})-{\bm{\mu}}
and
{\bf X}(Y-{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})
. In particular, we have
{\hbox{E}}\{h_{\psi}({\bf X},Y;{\bm{\theta}}^{*})\}={\bf 0}
for any
\psi
. Indeed, we show in Remark
2
that the optimal choice of
\psi
is
\psi=1
, which implies
h_{\psi}({\bf X},Y;{\bm{\theta}}^{*})={\bf E}
in view of (
5
). Thus, from a geometric perspective,
h_{\psi}({\bf X},Y;{\bm{\theta}})
is the residual by projecting the score function
{\bf X}(Y-{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}})
onto the set of unbiased estimating functions
{\bf X}m({\bf X})-{\bm{\mu}}
in the
L_{2}({\mathbb{P}})
norm. Following the insight from the above geometric interpretation, we now propose the dependable semi-supervised inference approach.

3.2
Dependable semi-supervised inference

To formulate the inference procedure, we first consider how to estimate the coefficient matrix
\mathbb{B}
. In view of (
5
) and the followup discussion, to estimate
\mathbb{B}
, we can either pre-specify a nonlinear function
m({\bf X})
or perhaps use a more flexible approach to estimate
m({\bf X})
from the data.
To see this, we can define
m({\bf X})=\argmin_{g\in{\mathcal{G}}}{\hbox{E}}\{Y-g({\bf X})\}^{2}
, where
{\mathcal{G}}
is a pre-specified class of functions of
{\bf X}
; e.g., class of linear functions, additive functions, functions correspond to interaction model
(
63
)
, single-index model
(
38
;
58
;
19
)
or multi-index model
(
57
)
.
Consider a concrete example where the choice of
{\mathcal{G}}=\{\sum_{j=1}^{p}\alpha_{j}X_{j}+\sum_{1\leq k<\ell\leq p}\beta_{k\ell}X_{\ell}X_{k}:\alpha_{j},\beta_{k\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}\}
corresponds to the class of functions with main effects and the second-order interactions. By fitting a penalized interaction model
(
63
)
, we can construct an estimator
\widehat{m}({\bf X})
.
In the rest of the paper, we assume an estimator
\widehat{m}({\bf X})
of
m({\bf X})
is available.
The detailed technical conditions on
\widehat{m}({\bf X})
are shown in Assumption
1
and Theorem
1
.
More discussions about other choices of class
{\mathcal{G}}
can be found in the Supplement
S.5.2
.
We apply a cross-fitting approach to estimate
\mathbb{B}
.
Given the estimator
\widehat{m}^{-j}(\cdot)
obtained from the labeled data
D^{*}\backslash D_{j}^{*}
for
j=1,2
, we can estimate the
k
th column of
\mathbb{B}
by

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbb{B}}^{j}_{\cdot k}=\arg\min_{{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}\Big[X_{ik}(Y_{i}-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{D})-{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}\{{\bf X}_{i}\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})-\widehat{\bm{\mu}}^{j}\}\Big]^{2}+\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}\norm{\bbeta}_{1},

(7)

where
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{D}
is the supervised Dantzig estimator in (
2
),
\widehat{\bm{\mu}}^{j}=\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D^{*}_{j}}\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i}){\bf X}_{i}
,
\lambda_{D}
and
\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}
are two tuning parameters. We note that it is possible to estimate
{\bm{\mu}}={\hbox{E}}\{{\bf X}m({\bf X})\}
by using both labeled and unlabeled data
D_{j}
. However, the rate of the estimator
\widehat{\mathbb{B}}^{j}_{\cdot k}
remains the same. The final estimator of
\mathbb{B}_{\cdot k}
is
\widehat{\mathbb{B}}_{\cdot k}=(\widehat{\mathbb{B}}^{1}_{\cdot k}+\widehat{\mathbb{B}}^{2}_{\cdot k})/2
, and this leads to
\widehat{\mathbb{B}}=(\widehat{\mathbb{B}}_{\cdot 1},...,\widehat{\mathbb{B}}_{\cdot p})
.

Motivated by the form of
\bar{\bm{\xi}}_{\psi}
in (
6
), we construct the following estimate of
\bm{\xi}={\hbox{E}}({\bf X}Y)
,

\widehat{\bm{\xi}}_{S,\psi}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\bf X}_{i}Y_{i}}{n}-\frac{\psi}{2}\widehat{\mathbb{B}}^{\rm T}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\left\{\frac{\sum_{i\in D^{*}_{j}}{\bf X}_{i}\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})}{n_{j}}-\frac{\sum_{i\in D_{j}}{\bf X}_{i}\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})}{n_{j}+N_{j}}\right\},

(8)

where we apply the cross-fitting technique again.
We note that different from the estimator
\widehat{\bm{\mu}}^{j}
used in
\widehat{\mathbb{B}}^{j}_{\cdot k}
, we estimate
{\bm{\mu}}
by
\frac{1}{n_{j}+N_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}}{\bf X}_{i}\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})
in (
8
), which incorporates the information from the unlabeled data.

Similar to
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
in (
4
), we propose the following dependable semi-supervised estimator

\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}=\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{D}+\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}(\widehat{\bm{\xi}}_{S,\psi}-\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{n}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{D}),

(9)

where
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{D}
is the supervised Dantzig estimator in (
2
),

\widehat{\bm{\xi}}_{S,\psi}
is defined in (
8
),
\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\bf X}_{i}^{\otimes 2}
and
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}
is the node-wise lasso estimator in (
S.11
). It is worthwhile to note that we estimate
{\bm{\Sigma}}
by
\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{n}
in (
9
), whereas we use
\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{n+N}=\frac{1}{n+N}\sum_{i=1}^{n+N}{\bf X}_{i}^{\otimes 2}
in the estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
. Indeed, this is a critical difference as replacing
\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{n}
with
\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{n+N}
in (
9
) corresponds to an estimating function different from
h_{\psi}({\bf X},Y;{\bm{\theta}})
and therefore no longer leads to a more efficient estimator.

3.3
Theory

To show the theoretical properties of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}
, we require the following assumptions.

Assumption
1
.

(E1)

The smallest eigenvalue of
{\hbox{E}}[\{{\bf X}m({\bf X})-{\bm{\mu}}\}^{\otimes 2}]
is lower bounded by a positive constant.
The 2nd moment of
E_{j}
in (
5
) is less than
C
and
|m({\bf X}_{i})|\leq C
, for some constant
C
.
The estimator
\widehat{m}^{-j}(\cdot)
satisfies
\norm{\wh m^{-j}-m}_{2}=O_{p}(c_{n})
for a deterministic sequence
c_{n}
.
We require
s_{B}K_{1}^{2}\Big(c_{n}+\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\Big)=o(1)
and
\sqrt{\frac{s\log p}{n}}=O(1)
.

(E2)

The columns of the matrix
\mathbb{B}
are sparse with
\max_{1\leq k\leq p}\norm{\bB_{\cdot k}}_{0}=s_{B}
and
\max_{1\leq k\leq p}\norm{\bB_{\cdot k}}_{1}

\leq L_{B}
for some
L_{B}
that may grow with
n
.

(E3)

\norm{\bOmega}_{\infty}\leq L_{\Omega}
and
(K_{1}L_{\Omega})^{2}s_{\Omega}\sqrt{\log p/(n+N)}=o(1)
, where
s_{\Omega}
is the maximum rowwise sparsity of
{\bm{\Omega}}
defined in Assumption
S.2
(presented in Supplement
S.3.1
).

(E4)

{\hbox{E}}|\epsilon|^{2+\delta}=O(1)
and
{\hbox{E}}|\eta|^{2+\delta}=O(1)
, where
\epsilon=Y-f({\bf X})
and
\eta=f({\bf X})-{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
.

Assumption (E1) guarantees that the restricted eigenvalue (RE) condition holds for the estimation of
\mathbb{B}_{\cdot k}
in (
7
).
Assumption (E2) is the sparsity assumption of
\mathbb{B}_{\cdot k}
. For example, in the ideal case that we choose
m({\bf X})=f({\bf X})-{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
and then
\mathbb{B}=\mathbb{I}_{p}
is sparse.
We note that there are other practical settings that the sparsity assumption of
\mathbb{B}_{\cdot k}
is reasonable (e.g.,
{\bf X}
is blockwise independent). We defer the detailed discussion to Supplement
S.5.1
.
In Assumption (E2), we further require that the matrix
L_{\infty}
norm of
\mathbb{B}
is bounded by
L_{B}
, which is used to establish the rate of
\widehat{\mathbb{B}}
.
Assumption (E3) and (A2) in Assumption
S.1
(presented in Supplement
S.3.1
) together imply the strong boundedness condition and
\max_{1\leq i\leq n+N}\max_{1\leq k\leq p}|{\bf X}_{i,-k}^{\rm T}{\bm{\gamma}}_{k}|=O(K)
in
47
which further guarantees the rate of
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}
in the matrix
L_{\infty}
norm.
In particular, to control the remainder term in the asymptotic expansion of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}
, we need
\norm{\bOmega}_{\infty}\leq L_{\Omega}
. Together with the boundedness assumption
\|{\bf X}_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq K_{1}
in Assumption
S.1
(presented in Supplement
S.3.1
), it implies
\|{\bm{\Omega}}{\bf X}_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq\norm{\bOmega}_{\infty}\|{\bf X}_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq K_{1}L_{\Omega}
.
Note that (E3) is equivalent to Assumption
S.2
(presented in Supplement
S.3.1
) by replacing
K_{1}L_{\Omega}
with
K
.
Assumption (E4) assumes that
\epsilon
and
\eta
have bounded
(2+\delta)
moment, which is used to simplify the Lyapunov condition.
Denote

{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}={\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i1}^{\otimes 2})-\frac{N(2\psi-\psi^{2})}{n+N}\{{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}{\bf T}_{i1}^{\rm T})\}^{\rm T}\{{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}^{\otimes 2})\}^{-1}{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}{\bf T}_{i1}^{\rm T}),

(10)

where
{\bf T}_{i1}={\bf X}_{i}(Y_{i}-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})
and
{\bf T}_{i2}={\bf X}_{i}m({\bf X}_{i})-{\bm{\mu}}
.

Theorem
1
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
and
S.1
(presented in Supplement
S.3.1
) hold. We choose
\lambda_{D}\asymp K_{1}\sqrt{\frac{(\sigma^{2}+\Phi^{2})\log p}{n}}
in (
2
),
\lambda_{k}\asymp K\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}
in (
S.9
) and
\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}=\widetilde{\lambda}_{opt}
in (
7
), where
\widetilde{\lambda}_{opt}
is defined in Proposition
S.2
. Then for any
{\bm{v}}\neq{\bf 0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
,
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})
equals

\displaystyle{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}\left[\frac{\mathbb{X}^{\rm T}({\bf Y}-\mathbb{X}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})}{n}-\psi\mathbb{B}^{\rm T}\left\{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\bf X}_{i}m({\bf X}_{i})}{n}-\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n+N}{\bf X}_{i}m({\bf X}_{i})}{n+N}\right\}\right]+O_{p}(\bar{\delta}_{n}),

(11)

where

\bar{\delta}_{n}=\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}L_{\Omega}K_{1}^{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\left\{L_{B}s_{\Omega}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}+s_{B}^{1/2}L_{B}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}+c_{n}\right)+K_{1}(s\vee s_{B})\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right\}

with
c_{n}
defined in Assumption
1
(E1) and
{\hbox{E}}[\{f({\bf X})-{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\}^{2}]\leq\Phi^{2}
. In addition, if
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}\geq C\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}^{2}
for some constant
C
,
\bar{\delta}_{n}/\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}=o(n^{-1/2})
and

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}L_{\Omega}K_{1}}{\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}}\Big)^{2+\delta}\frac{1}{n^{\delta/2}}\Big(1+\frac{L_{B}^{2+\delta}N}{n+N}\Big)=o(1),

(12)

then

n^{1/2}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})/({\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}})^{1/2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.

We now elaborate on the technical conditions used in Theorem
1
.
In
(
11
), the remainder term
\bar{\delta}_{n}
characterizes the effect of the plug-in estimators
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}
,
\widehat{\mathbb{B}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{D}
.
To establish the asymptotic normality of
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})
, we further need to assume that
\delta_{n}
is sufficiently small and the Lyapunov condition (
12
) holds so that one can apply the central limit theorem to the leading terms in (
11
).
To further simplify the conditions in Theorem
1
, one can assume
\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}/\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}
,
K_{1},L_{\Omega},L_{B}
are all of order
O(1)
.
As a result, (
12
) always holds and the condition
\bar{\delta}_{n}/\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}=o(n^{-1/2})
is implied by

\displaystyle s_{\Omega}{\frac{\log p}{\sqrt{n+N}}}+(s\vee s_{B}){\frac{\log p}{\sqrt{n}}}+s_{B}^{1/2}c_{n}\sqrt{\log p}=o(1).

(13)

Note that the condition
s({\log p}/{\sqrt{n}})=o(1)
implied by (
13
) is actually slightly stronger than the condition (
S.8
) required by the debiased estimator
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
, with details presented in Theorem
S.1
in Supplement
S.3.1
.
This is because
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{D}
is not only used as an initial estimator when constructing the one-step estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}
but also used as a plug-in estimator to estimate
\mathbb{B}
in (
7
). The error of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{D}
accumulates in the asymptotic expansion of
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})
, leading to the slow order
s({\log p}/{\sqrt{n}})
in (
13
). In terms of the rate of
\widehat{m}^{-j}
in the
L_{2}({\mathbb{P}})
norm, (
13
) requires
c_{n}=o\{(s_{B}\log p)^{-1/2}\}
, slightly stronger than the condition for
\widehat{f}^{-j}
in (
S.8
).
Notably,
the condition
\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}/\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}=O(1)
is not always satisfied; in this case, (
13
) becomes

\displaystyle\frac{\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}}{\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}}\left\{s_{\Omega}{\frac{\log p}{\sqrt{n+N}}}+(s\vee s_{B}){\frac{\log p}{\sqrt{n}}}+s_{B}^{1/2}c_{n}\sqrt{\log p}+n^{-\frac{\delta}{2(2+\delta)}}\right\}=o(1).

(14)

Remark
1
.

The bound in (
14
) requires that the ratio
\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}/\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}
not be too large, which excludes cases where
{\bm{v}}
contains many large entries (e.g.,
{\bm{v}}=(1,1,...,1)^{\rm T}
). This observation is consistent with
9
, which shows that the debiased estimator does not yield optimal confidence intervals for
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}
when
{\bm{v}}
is dense.

To illustrate situations where our results apply, first note that if
{\bm{v}}={\bm{e}}_{j}
, the
j
th basis vector in
{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
, then
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}=\widehat{\theta}^{d}_{S,\psi,j}
reduces to the estimator of
\theta^{d}_{S,\psi,j}
.
In this case, condition (
14
) reduces to (
13
).
Similar observations apply when
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
is a linear combination of
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
with
\|{\bm{v}}\|_{0}
fixed.
More generally, the set of vectors
{\bm{v}}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
satisfying (
14
) forms a cone
\Big\{\frac{\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}}{\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}}\leq t_{n}[s_{\Omega}{\frac{\log p}{\sqrt{n+N}}}+(s\vee s_{B}){\frac{\log p}{\sqrt{n}}}+s_{B}^{1/2}c_{n}\sqrt{\log p}+n^{-\frac{\delta}{2(2+\delta)}}]^{-1}\Big\}
for some
t_{n}=o(1)
.
Compared with
9
, which considered the debiased estimator for
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
with sparse
{\bm{v}}
, condition (
14
) may still hold when
{\bm{v}}
is approximately sparse, i.e., when it contains many small but nonzero entries.
Our results therefore remain applicable in such settings.

Remark
2

(Efficiency improvement and optimality)
.

When the linear model is correctly specified, i.e.,
f({\bf X})={\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
, we have
{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}{\bf T}_{i1}^{\rm T})={\bf 0}
and
{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}=\sigma^{2}{\bm{\Sigma}}
.
Thus, the asymptotic variance of
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}
reduces to
\sigma^{2}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}
, which agrees with that of the supervised debiased estimator.
In the following, we assume
{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}{\bf T}_{i1}^{\rm T})={\hbox{E}}\{{\bf X}^{\otimes 2}m({\bf X})\eta\}
is of full rank. Since
{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}^{\otimes 2})
is strictly positive definite by Assumption (E1), it implies that
\{{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}{\bf T}_{i1}^{\rm T})\}^{\rm T}\{{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}^{\otimes 2})\}^{-1}{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}{\bf T}_{i1}^{\rm T})
is strictly positive definite. We consider the following two cases.

(1)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{n}{n+N}=1
. Recall that the asymptotic variance of the supervised debiased estimator is
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}\mathbb{K}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}
, where
\mathbb{K}={\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i1}^{\otimes 2})
with
{\bf T}_{i1}={\bf X}_{i}(Y_{i}-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})
; see
7
. In this case, the asymptotic variance of
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}
is identical to
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}\mathbb{K}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}
. Thus there is no efficiency improvement when
n\gg N
.

(2)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{n}{n+N}=\rho
for some
0\leq\rho<1
.
In this case, the asymptotic variance
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}
is strictly smaller than
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}\mathbb{K}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}
if and only if
2\psi-\psi^{2}>0
, i.e.
0<\psi<2
.
Thus, our estimator
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}
with
0<\psi<2
is more efficient than the supervised estimator.
Interestingly, the asymptotic variance
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}
is minimized when taking
\psi=1
. Thus, the estimator
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi=1}
is optimal within the following class of estimators
\{{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}:\psi\in{\mathbb{R}}\}
in terms of asymptotic efficiency.
In view of the form of
{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}
, the variance reduction becomes more evident as
\rho
goes to
0
(i.e.,
N
increases).
In the interest of space, we defer more detailed discussions regarding the efficiency gain in Supplement
S.5.3
.

Remark
3

(Comparison with the estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
)
.

To see the connection of the two estimators
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
and
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}
in (
4
) and (
9
), consider the ideal case with
m({\bf X})=f({\bf X})-{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
. From (
10
), we can show that with
\psi=1
,

{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}=\sigma^{2}{\bm{\Sigma}}+{\hbox{E}}({\bf X}^{\otimes 2}\eta^{2})-\frac{N}{n+N}{\hbox{E}}({\bf X}^{\otimes 2}\eta^{2})=\sigma^{2}{\bm{\Sigma}}+\frac{n}{n+N}{\hbox{E}}({\bf X}^{\otimes 2}\eta^{2})
,
where
\eta=f({\bf X})-{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
. The asymptotic variances of
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
and
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}
are identical, since
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}={\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\sigma^{2}{\bm{\Omega}}+\frac{n}{n+N}{\bm{\Gamma}}){\bm{v}}
, where
{\bm{\Gamma}}={\bm{\Omega}}{\hbox{E}}({\bf X}^{\otimes 2}\eta^{2}){\bm{\Omega}}
. Thus, in the ideal case when
f({\bf X})
is known, using
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}
with
m({\bf X})=f({\bf X})-{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
would not suffer efficiency loss compared to
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
and both estimators improve the efficiency of the debiased estimator (and attains the semi-parametric efficiency bound under certain conditions); see Remark
2
above and Remark
S.2
(presented in Supplement
S.3.1
). However, if there is no sufficient information for us to estimate
f({\bf X})
consistently, the estimator
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
may not improve the efficiency of the debiased estimator, whereas
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}
does not rely on the estimation of
f({\bf X})
and guarantees the efficiency improvement for any
m({\bf X})
that satisfies the conditions in Theorem
1
. We note that the amount of efficiency improvement of
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}
depends on the choice of
m({\bf X})
. Unless we choose
m({\bf X})=f({\bf X})-{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
, the estimator
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}
in general would not attain the semi-parametric efficiency bound.

From Remarks
2
and
3
, we can see that the estimator
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{S,\psi}^{d}
provides a dependable use of the unlabeled data, since it is no worse than the supervised approach, no matter whether the linear model is correctly specified or the conditional mean function is consistently estimated.

As mentioned in the introduction, when the dimension
p
is fixed,
3
and
13
investigated how to incorporate the unlabeled data to improve the estimation efficiency for
\theta_{j}^{*}
. In addition to the technical challenges arise from the high dimensionality, the way we construct our estimator

\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}
is different from theirs. Unlike
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}
, their estimators cannot guarantee the efficiency improvement if the parameter of interest is the linear combination of
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
(e.g.,
\theta^{*}_{1}+\theta^{*}_{2}
). We refer to Supplement
S.5.5
for more details.

3.4
Variance estimation

We now consider how to estimate the asymptotic variance of
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}
.
To estimate
{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}
in (
10
), we note that
\widehat{\mathbb{B}}^{\rm T}
is an estimate of
{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}{\bf T}_{i1}^{\rm T})^{\rm T}\{{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}^{\otimes 2})\}^{-1}
. We can further estimate
\mathbb{M}_{1}={\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i1}^{\otimes 2})
and
\mathbb{M}_{2}={\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}{\bf T}_{i1}^{\rm T})
by

\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{1}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_{i}-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{D})^{2}{\bf X}_{i}^{\otimes 2}

and
\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}=(\widehat{\mathbb{M}}^{1}_{2}+\widehat{\mathbb{M}}^{2}_{2})/2
, where

\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}^{j}=\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D^{*}_{j}}(Y_{i}-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{D})\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i}){\bf X}_{i}^{\otimes 2}
.
Given these estimates, an estimator of
{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}
is defined as

\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}=\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{1}-\frac{N(2\psi-\psi^{2})}{n+N}\widehat{\mathbb{B}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
.

Proposition
1
.

Assume conditions in Theorem
1
,
{\hbox{E}}(\epsilon^{4})=O(1),{\hbox{E}}(\eta^{4})=O(1)
and
Rem=o(1)
, where

Rem=K_{1}(s_{B}+s_{B}^{1/2}L_{B})\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}+c_{n}\right)+K_{1}^{2}\sqrt{\frac{ss_{B}\log p}{n}}+K_{1}L_{B}\sqrt{\frac{s\log p}{n}}
.
Then,

\Big|{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}-{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}\Big|=O_{p}\Big\{\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2}(R_{1}+R_{2}+R_{3})\Big\},

(15)

where
R_{1}=K_{1}L_{\Omega}^{2}s_{\Omega}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}\|{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}\|_{\max}
,
R_{2}=K_{1}^{3}L_{\Omega}^{2}\sqrt{\frac{s\log p}{n}}
,
R_{3}=\frac{NK_{1}^{2}L_{\Omega}^{2}}{n+N}Rem
.
Thus, if
\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2}(R_{1}+R_{2}+R_{3})/\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}^{2}=o(1)
, we have

\displaystyle n^{1/2}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})/({\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}})^{1/2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{N}(0,1).

(16)

We note that the three terms
R_{1},R_{2}
and
R_{3}
in (
15
) stem from the estimation errors of
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}
,
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{D}
and
\widehat{\mathbb{B}}
, respectively. To further simplify the conditions in Proposition
1
, let us consider the case that
\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}/\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}
,
K_{1},L_{\Omega},L_{B}
and
\|{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}\|_{\max}
are all of order
O(1)
.
Then, the asymptotic normality in (
16
) is valid provided
(s\vee s_{B})\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}=o(1)
,
s_{\Omega}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}=o(1)
and
s_{B}c_{n}=o(1)
.

0:

D^{*}=\{({\bf X}_{i},Y_{i}):i=1,\cdots,n\}
from the label data,
U=\{{\bf X}_{i}:i=n+1,\cdots,n+N\}
from the unlabel data. Let
D=D^{*}\cup U
,
D^{*}=D_{1}^{*}\cup D^{*}_{2}
,
U=U_{1}\cup U_{2}
,
D_{1}=D_{1}^{*}\cup U_{1}
and
D_{2}=D_{2}^{*}\cup U_{2}
, with
|D_{j}^{*}|=n_{j}
and
|U_{j}|=N_{j}
,
j=1,2
.

0:

Estimators
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{SD}
,
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
and
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}
.

1:

Estimate
\widehat{f}^{-j}(\cdot)
and
\widehat{m}^{-j}(\cdot)
using data
D^{*}\backslash D^{*}_{j}
for
j=\{1,2\}
, respectively;

2:

Compute the estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{SD}
proposed in
18
via equation (
3
).

3:

Compute the straightforward debiased estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
via equation (
4
),
and obtain the confident intervals via equation (
S.14
).
Note that the details of the estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
are presented in Supplement
S.3.1
.

4:

Compute the proposed estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}
via equation (
9
), and obtain the confident intervals via equation (
17
).

Algorithm 1

The algorithm to compute the estimators
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{SD}
,
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
and
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}
via cross-fitting.

Lastly, from (
16
), we can construct the
(1-\alpha)
confidence interval for
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
as

\displaystyle[{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}-z_{1-\alpha/2}n^{-1/2}\widehat{sd},{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}+z_{1-\alpha/2}n^{-1/2}\widehat{sd}],

(17)

where
z_{1-\alpha/2}
is the
1-\alpha/2
quantile of a standard normal distribution and
\widehat{sd}=({\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}})^{1/2}
.
Similarly, if the interest is in testing the hypothesis
H_{0}:{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}=0
, we can construct the test statistic
n^{1/2}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}/({\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}})^{1/2}
based on (
16
).

3.5
Algorithm

For clarity, we summarize the algorithm to compute the estimators
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{SD}
,
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
and
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}
via cross-fitting in Algorithm
1
.

4
Extension

So far, we have only considered the situation that the parameter of interest
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
is defined as
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}=\arg\min_{{\bm{\theta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}{\hbox{E}}\{(Y-{\bf X}{{}^{\rm T}}{\bm{\theta}})^{2}\}
.
In this section, we extend our proposed methodology to the more general M-estimation framework; i.e.,

{\bm{\theta}}^{*}=\arg\min_{{\bm{\theta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}{\hbox{E}}\{L({\bf X},Y;{\bm{\theta}})\}
,
with
L({\bf X},Y;{\bm{\theta}})
twice continuously differentiable in
{\bm{\theta}}
,

{\hbox{E}}\{\nabla_{{\bm{\theta}}{\bm{\theta}}{{}^{\rm T}}}L({\bf X},Y;{\bm{\theta}}^{*})\}
positive definite, and that
{\hbox{E}}\{\nabla_{{\bm{\theta}}{\bm{\theta}}{{}^{\rm T}}}L({\bf X},Y;{\bm{\theta}})\}
being nonsingular for all
{\bm{\theta}}
in a neighborhood of
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
.
Clearly, the previously defined
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
corresponds to the situation that
\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}L({\bf X},Y;{\bm{\theta}})={\bf X}(Y-{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}})
, and the parameter of interest
{\bm{\beta}}
studied in
22
corresponds to the situation that
\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}L({\bf X},Y;{\bm{\theta}})={\bf X}\{Y-g({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}
with a known function
g(\cdot)
.

In this general M-estimation framework, the supervised Dantzig selector is defined as
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{M,D}=\arg\min_{{\bm{\theta}}}\|{\bm{\theta}}\|_{1}
s.t.
\Big\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}L({\bf X}_{i},Y_{i};{\bm{\theta}})\Big\|_{\infty}\leq\lambda_{M,D}

with
\lambda_{M,D}
a tuning parameter.
In our proposed methodology, we project the gradient
\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}L({\bf X},Y;{\bm{\theta}})
as

\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}L({\bf X},Y;{\bm{\theta}})=\mathbb{B}^{\rm T}\{{\bf m}({\bf X})-{\bm{\mu}}\}+{\bf E}
,
where
{\bf m}({\bf x})=\{m_{1}({\bf x}),\ldots,m_{p}({\bf x})\}^{\rm T}
,
{\bm{\mu}}={\hbox{E}}\{{\bf m}({\bf X})\}
, and the projection coefficient is

\mathbb{B}=\left[{\hbox{E}}\{{\bf m}({\bf X})-{\bm{\mu}}\}^{\otimes 2}\right]^{-1}{\hbox{E}}\left[\{{\bf m}({\bf X})-{\bm{\mu}}\}\,\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}^{\rm T}L({\bf X},Y;{\bm{\theta}})\right]
.
Next, we define the estimating function
h_{\psi}({\bf X},Y;{\bm{\theta}})=\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}L({\bf X},Y;{\bm{\theta}})-\psi\,\mathbb{B}^{\rm T}\{{\bf m}({\bf X})-{\bm{\mu}}\}

which has mean zero for any
\psi\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

Analogous to
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}
in (
9
), we define the proposed estimator here as

\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{M,S,\psi}=\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{M,D}-\left\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\nabla_{{\bm{\theta}}{\bm{\theta}}^{\rm T}}L({\bf X}_{i},Y_{i};\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{M,D})\right\}^{-1}\left\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}h_{\psi}({\bf X}_{i},Y_{i};\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{M,D})\right\}
,
where the last term
can be written as

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}L({\bf X}_{i},Y_{i};\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{M,D})-\psi\,\widehat{\mathbb{B}}^{\rm T}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\left\{\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}\widehat{\bf m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})-\frac{1}{n_{j}+N_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}}\widehat{\bf m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})\right\}
.
Similarly defining
{\bf T}_{i1}=\nabla_{{\bm{\theta}}}L({\bf X}_{i},Y_{i};{\bm{\theta}}^{*})
,
{\bf T}_{i2}={\bf m}({\bf X}_{i})-{\bm{\mu}}
and
{\bm{\Omega}}=\left\{{\hbox{E}}\nabla_{{\bm{\theta}}{\bm{\theta}}^{\rm T}}L({\bf X},Y;{\bm{\theta}}^{*})\right\}^{-1}
, one can easily develop the analogous result as Theorem
1
.
In the interest of space, we only provide a heuristic description of the extension here without presenting the full details.

5
Simulation Studies

5.1
Data generating models and practical implementation

We first generate a
p
-dimensional multivariate normal random vector
{\bf U}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,{\bm{\Sigma}})
with
\Sigma_{jk}=0.3^{|j-k|}
. We set the covariate
{\bf X}=(X_{1},...,X_{p})^{\rm T}
to be
X_{1}=|U_{1}|
and
X_{j}=U_{j}~\mathrm{for}~1<j\leq p
. The reason we take
X_{1}=|U_{1}|
is that this transformation implies
{\hbox{E}}(X_{1}^{k}X_{j})=0
for
j\neq 1
but the parameter
\theta^{*}_{1}
for centered
X_{1}
is nonzero.

We first consider a non-additive model and call it
Model 1
, that

Y=0.6(X_{1}+X_{2})^{2}+0.4X_{4}^{3}-X_{5}+2X_{6}+\epsilon
,
where
\epsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.
To calculate the corresponding regression parameter
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
under the working linear model, we first center
Y
and
X_{1}
so that their means are 0. By Proposition 4 in
7
, the support of
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
is
S=\{1,2,4,5,6\}
and the corresponding regression parameter
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
is
(1.48,1.04,0,1.2,-1,2,0,...,0)^{\rm T}
.

Before we proceed to illustrate the results, we discuss several practical implementation issues for the proposed methods.
To compute our optimal semi-supervised estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{SD}
in (
3
), we apply the group lasso with spline basis to estimate a sparse additive regression function
\widehat{f}

(
23
)
. To be specific, we use the cubic spline basis with degree of freedom
df=5
. To select the penalty parameter in group lasso and make computation easier, the BIC criterion is used; see Section 4 in
23
for the definition. After we derive the estimator
\widehat{f}
and subsequently
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}
, we modify the source code in the
flare
package to compute the Dantzig type estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{SD}
, where the tuning parameter
\lambda_{SD}
is selected by 5 fold cross-validation. Given the estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{SD}
, we can compute the one-step estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
in (
4
) for inference, where
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}
is obtained by the node-wise lasso using the
glmnet
package with tuning parameter selected by 5 fold cross-validation.

To implement the dependable semi-supervised method, we choose
\widehat{m}(\cdot)=\widehat{f}(\cdot)
the estimated sparse additive function obtained previously. We estimate each column of the coefficient matrix
\mathbb{B}
by (
7
) using lasso with tuning parameters selected by cross-validation. With the optimal choice
\psi=1
(see Remark
2
), we can compute the dependable semi-supervised estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi=1}
in (
9
), where
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}
is obtained previously and the Dantzig selector
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{D}
is computed using the
flare
package.

To compare the inference results, we consider two versions of debiased lasso estimators,

\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}^{d}=\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{lasso}+\bar{\bm{\Omega}}\Big(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\bf X}_{i}Y_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{n}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{lasso}\Big),~~\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{2}^{d}=\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{lasso}+\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}\Big(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\bf X}_{i}Y_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{n}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{lasso}\Big),

(18)

where
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{lasso}
and
\bar{\bm{\Omega}}
are the standard lasso and node-wise lasso estimator applied to the labeled data. The only difference between
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}^{d}
and
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{2}^{d}
is the way of estimating the precision matrix
{\bm{\Omega}}
. The two estimators
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}^{d},\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{2}^{d}
and the associated confidence intervals can be computed using the
hdi
package with Robust option.

5.2
Numerical results

With sample size
n\in\{100,300,500\}
, the ratio
N/n\in\{1,4,8\}
and the dimension
p\in\{200,500\}
, we compare the performance of the four methods:
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{1}
(D-Lasso1, that only uses labeled data with sample size
n
),
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{2}
(D-Lasso2), both defined in (
18
), the straightforward debiased estimator D-SSL
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
defined in (
4
), and the proposed dependable semi-supervised estimator S-SSL
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi=1}
defined in (
9
).

For the
p=200
case, we report the empirical bias (Bias), standard deviation (SD), root mean squared error (RMSE) and the half length of 95% confidence interval (len/2) for each of the single parameters
\theta_{1}
,
\theta_{2}
,
\theta_{4}
,
\theta_{5}
and
\theta_{6}
in Table
1
, and plot the absolute difference between the empirical 95% coverage probability and the nominal level 0.95 in Figure
1
.
In the interest of space, the corresponding results for the
p=500
case are placed in Table
S.1
and Figure
S.1
in the Supplement.
These results are based on 100 simulation replications.
In Table
2
, we also report the computation time (in seconds) of one simulation replication of these four methods for both
p=200
and
p=500
.

From these results, in the majority of the scenarios we consider, the proposed method S-SSL has the smallest SD and RMSE, compared to the methods D-Lasso1 and D-Lasso2.
This shows, even with a misspecified conditional mean function modeling strategy, S-SSL can still achieve efficiency gain, indicating the dependable use of the unlabeled data.
The coverage rate of the method S-SSL is close to the nominal level, especially when the sample size increases to
n=500
.
However, the method D-SSL has a low coverage rate in some cases.
This results from a poor estimation of conditional mean as the true model is no longer additive.
Computing the proposed method S-SSL takes a slightly longer time than all other methods, due to its sophisticated nature.
An interesting phenomenon we observe in this comparison is that computation takes longer when
n
is approximately equal to
p
, but decreases once
n
becomes larger than
p
.
In practice, estimation of the conditional mean function can be difficult especially under high-dimensionality, therefore we recommend S-SSL as it provides a dependable use of unlabeled data even if the imposed conditional mean model is incorrect.

We also conduct similar numerical investigations when the data generating model is additive and we call it
Model 2
.
In the interest of space, we defer the detailed results to Supplement
S.6
.
Besides the parallel results in Table
S.2
, Table
S.3
and Table
S.4
, we also conduct different sensitivity analyses with different condition mean function estimation methods, different tuning parameter selection methods, and different estimands; see Table
S.5
, Table
S.6
, Table
S.7
and Table
S.8
in the Supplement for details.
