6
Real Data Application

In this section, we apply our proposed method to a real-world dataset from the Medical Information Mart for Intensive Care III (MIMIC-III) database
(
25
)
.
MIMIC-III is an openly available electronic health records system developed by the MIT Lab for Computational Physiology.
It comprises deidentified health-related data associated with intensive care unit patients with rich information including demographics, vital signs, laboratory test, medications, and so on.
Our initial motivation for this data analysis is the association study for the albumin level in the blood sample, a very indicative biomarker correlated with the phenotypes of different types of diseases
(
37
)
.
We focus on a subset with 4784 patients of the whole database that the albumin level is available.

Some data cleaning strategy is inevitable for handling electronic health records database. In our situation, around 54% covariates contain missing values. Among these covariates with missing values, the missingness proportions are 9.4% on average and the range is from 0.2% to 30.8%.
For those missing values, we simply impute them using the mean of observed samples, the so-called mean imputation. For many clinical markers with continuous scale, the database collects the minimum, the maximum, as well as the mean, values across a certain period of time. To alleviate the potential collinearity among these variables but also to maintain as much information as possible, we decide to only include the maximum and the mean values in our analysis. Additionally, we convert the categorical variables, such as gender and marital status, to dummy variables.
The number of features after data pre-processing is
p=162
. We randomly sample 4500 observations out of 4784 patients and divide them into
n=500
labeled data and
N=4000
unlabeled data, where the value of the outcome variable
albumin
is removed for the 4000 unlabeled data.
From the set of 500 labeled instances, a subset of 100 is chosen as the observed labeled data. We then construct unlabeled sample sets by selecting the top 1,000, 2,000, and 3,000 instances from the unlabeled pool, in order to study the effects of varying the unlabeled data size.

Firstly, following the simulation setup, we use the
hdi
package with the
robust
option to obtain the debiased Lasso estimator (D‑Lasso1) from the labeled data. Due to multiple testing, the p-value for each covariate is corrected using the default
holm
approach. We then implemented the proposed D-SSL and S-SSL procedures using the same configuration as in the simulation design and computed the Holm-adjusted
p
-values. For reference, we also obtained a supervised debiased Lasso estimator based on all entire dataset, which is called “Oracle” and also computed the Holm-adjusted
p
-values.

We focused on four clinically and biologically relevant biomarkers,

Total Calcium
(TC),
Free Calcium
(FC),
Iron Binding Capacity
(IBC) and
Red Cell Distribution Width

(RDW), and evaluated three sample-size configurations,
(n,N)\in\{(100,1000),(100,2000),(100,3000)\}
. Figure
2
summarizes the results. All three proposed methods consistently identified TC, a well-established biochemical marker reflecting overall calcium status
(
36
)
. For FC, D-SSL and S-SSL correctly recovered the signal, whereas D-Lasso1 failed to detect it.
Free calcium represents the ionized, physiologically active component of serum calcium and therefore carries strong biological relevance
(
4
)
.
IBC was selected only by S-SSL; this marker reflects transferrin-mediated iron transport and is central to assessing iron metabolism
(
11
)
.
Finally, RDW, which quantifies the heterogeneity of red blood cell size
(
41
;
35
)
, may not be detected by D-SSL at smaller sample sizes but was recovered once more unlabeled data became available. All three methods ultimately selected RDW as
N
increased.
Notably, S-SSL consistently identified all four variables across settings, demonstrating superior detection power.

The confidence intervals exhibit a clear efficiency pattern. For variables selected by S-SSL, their intervals are uniformly shorter than those of D-Lasso1, indicating substantial efficiency gains from incorporating unlabeled data. By contrast, the performance of D-SSL varies across covariates: its intervals can be shorter, comparable, or wider; reflecting the fact that its efficiency improvement depends on correct specification of the working conditional mean model
f(\cdot)
and is not guaranteed under misspecification.

Figure 2
:
Real Data Application Results: the point estimates and the corresponding confidence intervals of the methods, Oracle, D-Lasso1, D-SSL, and S-SSL, with sample size
n=100
and
N\in\{1000,2000,3000\}
.

7
Discussion

In this paper, we propose the semi-supervised estimator

{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}
for
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
with a pre-specified
{\bm{v}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
.
This allows for the development of inference procedures for the general estimand
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
, such as constructing confidence intervals and conducting hypothesis tests.

A key advantage of the proposed estimator is that it guarantees performance no worse than the supervised approach, ensuring the dependable use of high-dimensional unlabeled data, while avoiding the need to estimate the true conditional mean function
f({\bf X})
.

Our framework is more suitable when the goal is to understand the relationship between
Y
and
{\bf X}
without knowledge of the true regression function, such as when investigating the association between a phenotype and genome-wide SNPs in a genome-wide association study.
In recent literature, there has been a surge of work defining model-agnostic measures to quantify the importance of covariates for prediction and studying their theoretical properties; these are commonly referred to as variable or feature importance measures
(
54
;
55
;
49
;
48
)
. Beyond regression settings, these ideas have also been extended to survival analysis
(
56
)
and causal inference
(
21
)
.
Our proposal shares a similar spirit with the variable importance literature in that both aim to understand the role of covariates in a model-agnostic framework. However, the two approaches are fundamentally different. Due to space limitations, we defer a detailed discussion of their similarities and differences to Supplement
S.5.6
.

Finally,
in some applications, labeled and unlabeled data are collected under different conditions or from distinct populations.
In such cases, assuming that the marginal distribution of
{\bf X}
differs between labeled and unlabeled data
(
26
)
is more appropriate. However, how such distributional differences impact the utility of unlabeled data for estimation and inference remains an open question.
We plan to explore this issue in future research.

SUPPLEMENT

S.1
Preliminary Definition

To characterize the tail behavior of random variables, we introduce the following definition.

Definition
S.1

(Sub-Gaussian variable and vector)
.

A random variable
X
is called sub-Gaussian if there exists some positive constant
K_{2}
such that
{\mathbb{P}}(|X|>t)\leq\exp(1-t^2/K^2_2)
for all
t\geq 0
. The sub-Gaussian norm of
X
is defined as
\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}=\sup_{q\geq 1}q^{-1/2}({\hbox{E}}|X|^{q})^{1/q}
. A vector
{\bf X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
is a sub-Gaussian vector if the one-dimensional marginals
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}
are sub-Gaussian for all
{\bm{v}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
, and its sub-Gaussian norm is defined as
\|{\bf X}\|_{\psi_{2}}=\sup_{\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}=1}\|{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}\|_{\psi_{2}}
.

S.2
Preliminary Lemmas

We start with several basic lemmas that we will apply in our proofs.

Lemma
S.1

(Lemma B.1 in
17
)
.

Let
\{X_{n}\},\{Y_{n}\}
be sequences of random variables. If for any
c>0
,
{\mathbb{P}}(|X_{n}|>c|Y_{n})=o_{p}(1)
. Then
X_{n}=o_{p}(1)
.

Lemma
S.2

(Nemirovski moment inequality, Lemma 14.24 in
6
)
.

For
m\geq 1
and
p>e^{m-1}
, we have

{\hbox{E}}\left(\max_{1\leq k\leq p}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\gamma_{k}(Z_{i})-{\hbox{E}}\{\gamma_{k}(Z_{i})\}\right]\right|^{m}\right)\leq(8\log 2p)^{\frac{m}{2}}{\hbox{E}}\left[\left\{\max_{1\leq k\leq p}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\gamma_{k}^{2}(Z_{i})\right\}^{m/2}\right]

(S.1)

Lemma
S.3

(Theorem 3.1 in
40
)
.

Assume that
\mathbb{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
has zero mean and covariance
{\bm{\Sigma}}
. Furthermore, assume that the rows of
\mathbb{X}{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
are independent sub-gaussian random vector with a bounded sub-gaussian constant and
\Lambda_{\min}({\bm{\Sigma}})>C_{\min}>0
,
\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\Sigma_{jj}=O(1)
. Set
0<\delta<1
,
0<s_{0}<p
, and
L>0
. Define the following event,

\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(n,s_{0},L)=\left\{\mathbb{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}:(1-\delta)\sqrt{C_{\min}}\leq\frac{\norm{\bX\bv}_{2}}{\sqrt{n}\norm{\bv}_{2}},\forall{\bm{v}}\in\mathcal{C}(s_{0},L)~\textrm{s.t.}~{\bm{v}}\neq 0\right\}.

(S.2)

and
\mathcal{C}(s_{0},L)=\{{\bm{\theta}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}:\exists S\subseteq\{1,...,p\},|S|=s_{0},\norm{\bt_{S^c}}_{1}\leq L\norm{\bt_S}_{1}\}
. Then, there exists a constant
c_{1}=c(L,\delta)
such that, for sample size
n\geq c_{1}s_{0}\log(p/s_0)
, we have

{\mathbb{P}}\left\{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(n,s_{0},L)\right\}\geq 1-e^{-\delta^{2}n}.

(S.3)

S.3
Theoretical Properties of the Debiased Estimator

This section is divided into four parts: Section
S.3.1
presents the central limit theorem for the Debiased Estimator. Section
S.3.2
discusses the variance estimation of the Debiased Estimator. Section
S.3.3
provides the proof of the central limit theorem for the estimator, and Section
S.3.4
examines the convergence rate of the variance estimate for the Debiased Estimator.

S.3.1
Asymptotic Properties of Debiased Estimator

To show the asymptotic distribution of the proposed estimator
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
, we require the following assumptions.

Assumption
S.1
.

(A1)

{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}{\bf X}
is a zero mean sub-gaussian vector with bounded sub-gaussian norm and
{\rm{Cov}}({\bf X})={\bm{\Sigma}}
has smallest eigenvalue
\Lambda_{\min}({\bm{\Sigma}})\geq C_{\min}>0
for some positive constant
C_{\min}
. Moreover,
\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\Sigma_{jj}=O(1)
.

(A2)

\max_{1\leq i\leq n+N}\norm{\X_i}_{\infty}\leq K_{1}
where we allow
K_{1}
to diverge with
(n,N,p)
.

(A3)

{\hbox{E}}(\epsilon^{2})=\sigma^{2}
and
{\hbox{E}}[\{f({\bf X})-{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\}^{2}]\leq\Phi^{2}
.

(A4)

{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
is
s
-sparse with
\norm{\bt^*}_{0}=s
, and
\frac{s\log p}{n+N}=O(1)
.

Assumption
S.2
.

Assume
\max_{1\leq i\leq n+N}\norm{\bOmega\X_i}_{\infty}\leq K_{2}
, and
\max_{1\leq k\leq p}\norm{\bOmega_{k\cdot}}_{0}\leq s_{\Omega}
satisfies

K^{2}s_{\Omega}\sqrt{\log p/(n+N)}=o(1)
, where
K=K_{1}\vee K_{2}
with
K_{1}
defined in Assumption
S.1
.

Note that Assumption
S.1
is the same as the Assumption 3.1 in
18
.
Assumption
S.2
and (A2) in Assumption
S.1
together imply the strong boundedness condition and
\max_{1\leq i\leq n+N}\max_{1\leq k\leq p}|{\bf X}_{i,-k}^{\rm T}{\bm{\gamma}}_{k}|=O(K)
in
47
which further guarantees the rate of
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}
in the matrix
L_{\infty}
norm. While it is possible to relax the sparsity assumption
\max_{1\leq k\leq p}\norm{\bOmega_{k\cdot}}_{0}\leq s_{\Omega}

(
24
)
, we make this assumption in order to show the proposed estimator is regular and asymptotically linear, which facilitates the comparison with other competing estimators in terms of asymptotic efficiency.
Finally, we note that Assumptions
S.1
and
S.2
do not impose or imply any upper bound on
\Lambda_{\max}({\bm{\Sigma}})
. For example, we allow
{\bm{\Sigma}}
to be an equicorrelation matrix, whose largest eigenvalue is proportional to the dimension
p
. Given these assumptions, the following theorem shows that
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
is asymptotically normal for a linear functional
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
.

Theorem
S.1
.

Suppose Assumptions
S.1
and
S.2
hold. By choosing
\lambda_{SD}\asymp K_{1}(\Phi\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}+\sigma\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}+b_{n}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}})
and
\lambda_{k}\asymp K\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}
uniformly over
k
, we obtain that for any
{\bm{v}}\neq{\bf m}{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
,

\displaystyle{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bf W}_{i}\{Y_{i}-f({\bf X}_{i})\}+\frac{1}{n+N}\sum_{i=1}^{n+N}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bf W}_{i}\{f({\bf X}_{i})-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\}+O_{p}(\delta_{n}),

(S.4)

where
{\bf W}_{i}={\bm{\Omega}}{\bf X}_{i}
and
\delta_{n}=\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}(R_{1}+R_{2})
with

R_{1}=K_{1}K(s\vee s_{\Omega})\left\{{\frac{\Phi\log p}{n+N}}+{\frac{(\sigma+b_{n})\log p}{\sqrt{n(n+N)}}}\right\},~~R_{2}=K_{2}b_{n}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}},

and
b_{n}
is a deterministic sequence that satisfies
\|\widehat{f}^{-j}-f\|=O_{p}(b_{n})
for
j=1,2
. In addition, if

\displaystyle\frac{n^{1/2}\delta_{n}}{\left\{{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\sigma^{2}{\bm{\Omega}}+\frac{n}{n+N}{\bm{\Gamma}}){\bm{v}}\right\}^{1/2}}=o(1)

(S.5)

with
{\bm{\Gamma}}={\hbox{E}}[{\bf W}_{i}^{\otimes 2}\{f({\bf X}_{i})-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\}^{2}]
,
\epsilon
and
\eta({\bf X})=f({\bf X})-{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
satisfy

\displaystyle\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2+\delta}K_{2}^{2+\delta}\left\{\frac{{\hbox{E}}|\epsilon|^{2+\delta}}{n^{\delta/2}(\sigma^{2}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}})^{1+\delta/2}}+\frac{{\hbox{E}}|\eta({\bf X})|^{2+\delta}}{(n+N)^{\delta/2}({\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Gamma}}{\bm{v}})^{1+\delta/2}}\right\}=o(1),

(S.6)

for some
\delta>0
, then

\displaystyle\frac{n^{1/2}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})}{\left\{{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\sigma^{2}{\bm{\Omega}}+\frac{n}{n+N}{\bm{\Gamma}}){\bm{v}}\right\}^{1/2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{N}(0,1).

(S.7)

The asymptotic expansion of
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})
is presented in (
S.4
), where the remainder term
\delta_{n}
consists of two components
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
, which come from the cross product of the estimation errors of
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{SD}
in Theorem 3.2 of
18
and the plug-in error of
\widehat{f}^{-j}
in
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}
, respectively. To establish the asymptotic normality of
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})
, we further need to assume that
\delta_{n}
is sufficiently small and the Lyapunov condition holds so that one can apply the central limit theorem to the leading terms in (
S.4
). These two conditions are rigorously formulated in (
S.5
) and (
S.6
). To further simplify (
S.5
) and (
S.6
), assume that
\sigma^{2}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}\geq C\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}^{2}
and
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Gamma}}{\bm{v}}\geq C\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}^{2}
for some constant
C
,
{\hbox{E}}|\epsilon|^{2+\delta},{\hbox{E}}|\eta({\bf X})|^{2+\delta},K
are all
O(1)
and
b_{n}=o(1)
. Under these mild conditions, (
S.5
) and (
S.6
) are implied by

\displaystyle\frac{\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}}{\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}}\left\{\frac{(s\vee s_{\Omega})\log p}{\sqrt{n+N}}+b_{n}\sqrt{\log p}+n^{-\frac{\delta}{2(2+\delta)}}\right\}=o(1).

(S.8)

Remark
S.1
.

(1) The bound (
S.8
) requires the ratio
\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}/\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}
cannot be too large which excludes the case that
{\bm{v}}
has many large entries (e.g.,
{\bm{v}}=(1,1,...,1)^{\rm T}
). This observation agrees with the theoretical results in
9
, as the debiased estimator does not yield optimal confidence intervals for
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
when
{\bm{v}}
is a dense vector. To see some concrete examples that our results are applicable, we first note that if
{\bm{v}}={\bm{e}}_{j}
the
j
th basis vector in
{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
, then
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}=\widehat{\theta}^{d}_{j}
reduces to the estimate of
\theta_{j}
. Our condition (
S.8
) becomes
(s\vee s_{\Omega})\log p=o(\sqrt{n+N})
and
b_{n}=o(1/\sqrt{\log p})
. The former is a standard condition for debiased inference adapted to the semi-supervised setting and the latter is slightly stronger than the consistency of
\widehat{f}^{-j}
required in Theorem 3.2 of
18
; see Remark 3.3 for details. The same comments are applicable if the parameter of interest
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
is a linear combination of
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
with
\|{\bm{v}}\|_{0}
fixed.

Indeed, the set of vector
{\bm{v}}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
satisfying (
S.8
) forms a cone
[\frac{\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}}{\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}}\leq t_{n}\{\frac{(s\vee s_{\Omega})\log p}{\sqrt{n+N}}+b_{n}\sqrt{\log p}+n^{-\frac{\delta}{2(2+\delta)}}\}^{-1}]
for some
t_{n}=o(1)
. Compared to
9
who proposed the debiased estimator for
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
with sparse
{\bm{v}}
, the cone condition (
S.8
) may still hold if
{\bm{v}}
is approximately sparse with many small but nonzero entries. Our results are still applicable in this case.

(2) Assuming
\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}/\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}
is a constant and
N\gg n
, we can see from (
S.8
) that in the semi-supervised setting we need
(s\vee s_{\Omega})\log p=o(\sqrt{n+N})
, which is much weaker than the similar condition
(s\vee s_{\Omega})\log p=o(\sqrt{n})
for the supervised estimators (up to some logarithmic factors). Thus, with a large amount of unlabeled data, our inference results may still hold for models with large
s
.

Remark
S.2

(Efficiency improvement and semi-parametric efficiency bound)
.

We first note that, when the linear model is correctly specified i.e.
f({\bf X})={\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
, we have
{\bm{\Gamma}}={\bf 0}
and the asymptotic variance of
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
reduces to
\sigma^{2}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}
, which agrees with the asymptotic variance of the debiased estimator in fully supervised setting and also matches the semi-parametric efficiency bound. In this case, the information of
X
contained in the unlabeled data is ancillary and does not contribute to the inference on
{\bm{\theta}}
; see also
3
,
13
.

In the following, we assume
{\bm{\Gamma}}
is strictly positive definite. Recall that our asymptotic analysis requires
n,p\rightarrow\infty
and allows
N
to be either fixed or grow with
n
. In the following, we discuss the asymptotic variance of
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
in (
S.8
) according to the magnitude of
N
.

(1)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{n}{n+N}=1
. Denote
\mathbb{K}={\hbox{E}}\left\{{\bf X}^{\otimes 2}(Y-X^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})^{2}\right\}
. It is seen that
\mathbb{K}=\sigma^{2}{\bm{\Sigma}}+{\bm{\Sigma}}{\bm{\Gamma}}{\bm{\Sigma}}
. In this case, the asymptotic variance of
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
reduces to
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\sigma^{2}{\bm{\Omega}}+{\bm{\Gamma}}){\bm{v}}={\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}\mathbb{K}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}
, which is the asymptotic variance of the debiased estimator in the fully supervised setting; see
7
,
34
. As expected, when
N\ll n
, the amount of unlabeled data is not sufficiently large to improve the asymptotic efficiency of the estimator.

(2)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{n}{n+N}=\rho
for some
0<\rho<1
. In this case, the asymptotic variance
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\sigma^{2}{\bm{\Omega}}+\rho{\bm{\Gamma}}){\bm{v}}
is strictly smaller than
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}\mathbb{K}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}={\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\sigma^{2}{\bm{\Omega}}+{\bm{\Gamma}}){\bm{v}}
. Thus, the unlabeled data can be used to improve the asymptotic efficiency for inference.

(3)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{n}{n+N}=0
. In the case, the asymptotic variance becomes
\sigma^{2}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}
. Indeed, if the distribution of
{\bf X}
is known, the semi-parametric efficiency bound for estimating
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
is exactly
\sigma^{2}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}
as well; see
13
and the reference therein. Thus, when
N\gg n
, our estimator attains the semi-parametric efficiency bound.

S.3.2
Variance Estimation

In the following, we consider how to estimate the asymptotic variance of
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
.

For estimating
{\bm{\Omega}}
,
one can consider the following node-wise lasso estimator
(
30
)
based on both labeled and unlabeled data
\widetilde{\mathbb{X}}
. For
k\in[p]
, define the vector
\widehat{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{k}=\{\widehat{\gamma}_{k,j}:j\in[p]~\textrm{and}~j\neq k\}
as

\widehat{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{k}=\argmin_{\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\left\{\frac{1}{n+N}||\mathbb{X}_{\cdot k}-\widetilde{\mathbb{X}}_{\cdot-k}{\bm{\gamma}}||_{2}^{2}+2\lambda_{k}\norm{\bgamma}_{1}\right\}.

(S.9)

Denote by

\widehat{\mathbb{C}}=\begin{bmatrix}1&-\widehat{\gamma}_{1,2}&\dots&-\widehat{\gamma}_{1,p}\\
-\widehat{\gamma}_{2,1}&1&\dots&-\widehat{\gamma}_{2,p}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
-\widehat{\gamma}_{p,1}&-\widehat{\gamma}_{p,2}&\dots&1\end{bmatrix}

and let

\widehat{\mathbb{T}}^{2}={\rm{diag}}(\widehat{\tau}_{1}^{2},...,\widehat{\tau}_{p}^{2}),~\textrm{where}~\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{2}=\frac{1}{n+N}(\widetilde{\mathbb{X}}_{\cdot k}-\widetilde{\mathbb{X}}_{\cdot-k}\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}_{k})^{\rm T}\widetilde{\mathbb{X}}_{\cdot k}.

(S.10)

Then the node-wise lasso estimator is defined as

\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}=\widehat{\mathbb{T}}^{-2}\widehat{\mathbb{C}}.

(S.11)

To estimate
\sigma^{2}
, we apply the cross-fitting technique. Specifically, for
j=\{1,2\}
, define

\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{j}=\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D^{*}_{j}}\left\{Y_{i}-\widehat{f}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})\right\}^{2}.

We estimate
\sigma^{2}
by
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}=(\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{1}+\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{2})/2
. Similarly, define

\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{j}=\frac{1}{n_{j}+N_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}}(\widehat{\eta}_{i}^{-j})^{2}\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}{\bf X}_{i}{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\Omega}},

where
\widehat{\eta}_{i}^{-j}=\widehat{f}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})-\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{SD}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i}
and
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}
is defined in (
S.11
). We then estimate
{\bm{\Gamma}}
by
\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}=(\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{1}+\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{2})/2
. The following Proposition shows that the asymptotic variance of
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
can be consistently estimated by the plug-in estimator
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\sigma}^{2}\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}+\frac{n}{n+N}\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}){\bm{v}}
.

Proposition
S.1
.

Suppose Assumptions
S.1
and
S.2
hold. To simplify the presentation, we further assume
{\hbox{E}}(\epsilon^{4})=O(1),{\hbox{E}}\left\{\eta^{4}({\bf X})\right\}=O(1)
and
K\sqrt{\frac{s\log p}{n+N}}=o(1)
.
Then

\left|{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\left(\widehat{\sigma}^{2}\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}+\frac{n}{n+N}\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}\right){\bm{v}}-{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\left(\sigma^{2}{\bm{\Omega}}+\frac{n}{n+N}{\bm{\Gamma}}\right){\bm{v}}\right|=O_{p}\left\{\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}^{2}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}+b_{n}^{2}\right)+\textrm{Rem}_{N}\right\},

(S.12)

where

\textrm{Rem}_{N}=\frac{n}{n+N}K^{2}\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2}b_{n}+K^{3}\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2}(s\vee s_{\Omega})\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}.

(S.13)

Under the additional assumptions
\sigma^{2}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}\geq C\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}^{2}
and
\textrm{Rem}_{N}/\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}^{2}=o(1)
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{n^{1/2}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})}{\left\{{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\sigma}^{2}\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}+\frac{n}{n+N}\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}){\bm{v}}\right\}^{1/2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{N}(0,1).

(S.14)

To better understand the convergence rate of the estimated asymptotic variance, we decompose the error in (
S.12
) into two terms,
\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}^{2}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}+b_{n}^{2}\right)
and
\textrm{Rem}_{N}
. The former is due to the estimation error of
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}
and the latter comes from the error of
\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}
. It is of interest to note that, if
N\gg n
, the error term
\textrm{Rem}_{N}
may vanish to 0 fast enough, so that the convergence rate of the estimated asymptotic variance in (
S.12
) is dominated by
\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}^{2}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}+b_{n}^{2}\right)
. In addition, for many practical estimators
\widehat{f}^{-j}
, such as the group lasso estimator for sparse additive models in Remark 3.3 of
18
, its convergence rate in
L_{2}({\mathbb{P}})
norm is no slower than
n^{-1/4}
, that is
b_{n}=o(n^{-1/4})
. In this case, the rate in (
S.12
) further reduces to
\|{\bm{v}}\|^{2}_{2}/\sqrt{n}
, which is the best possible rate for estimating the variance even if
{\bm{\Omega}}
,
{\bm{\Gamma}}
and
f(X)
are known. Thus, the unlabeled data lead to a more accurate estimate of the asymptotic variance.

Finally, from (
S.14
) we can construct the
(1-\alpha)
confidence interval for
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
as
[{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}-z_{1-\alpha/2}n^{-1/2}sd,{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}+z_{1-\alpha/2}n^{-1/2}sd]
, where
z_{1-\alpha/2}
is the
1-\alpha/2
quantile of a standard normal distribution and
sd=\Big\{{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\sigma}^{2}\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}+\frac{n}{n+N}\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}){\bm{v}}\Big\}^{1/2}
. Similarly, if one is interested in testing the hypothesis
H_{0}:{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}=0
, we can construct the test statistic
n^{1/2}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}/\left\{{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\sigma}^{2}\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}+\frac{n}{n+N}\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}){\bm{v}}\right\}^{1/2}
based on (
S.14
).

S.3.3
Proof of Theorem
S.1

Proof.

We will first derive some preliminary probability bounds that will be used later in the proof. With (A1)-(A5) in Assumptions (
S.1
) and (
S.2
), we can verify the assumptions (B1)-(B4) for strongly bounded case in Theorem 2.4 of
47
holds with
K=K_{1}\vee K_{2}
. In particular, we have

\displaystyle|\{{\bf X}^{(-k)}\}^{\rm T}\gamma_{k}|

\displaystyle=|\{{\bf X}^{(-k)}\}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Sigma}}_{-k,-k}^{-1}{\bm{\Sigma}}_{-k,k}|=|\{{\bf X}^{(-k)}\}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}_{-k,k}||{\bm{\Omega}}_{kk}^{-1}|

\displaystyle=|\{{\bf X}^{(-k)}\}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}_{-k,k}|({\bm{\Sigma}}_{kk}-{\bm{\Sigma}}_{k,-k}{\bm{\Sigma}}_{-k,-k}^{-1}{\bm{\Sigma}}_{-k,k})=O(K_{2}),

uniformly over
1\leq k\leq p
.

Under the the strongly bounded case with
s_{\Omega}=o\left(\frac{n+N}{\log p}\right)
, and
\max_{k}{\bm{\Sigma}}_{k,k}=O(1)
, we can apply Theorem 2.4 and Lemma 5.3 in
47

and claim that the nodewise lasso estimator satisfies

\norm{\wh\bOmega-\bOmega}_{\infty}=O_{p}\left(Ks_{\Omega}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}\right),~~\norm{\bI_p-\wh\bOmega\wh\bSigma_{n+N}}_{\max}=O_{p}\left(K\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}\right).

(S.15)

The first probability bound in (
S.15
) is directly from Theorem 2.4 of
47
. To see the second probability bound, with the formulation of
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}
and notation from nodewise Lasso (
S.10
), we know for each row of
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}
,

\norm{\wh\bSigma_{n+N}\bOmega_{k\cdot}\trans-e_k}_{\infty}\leq\lambda_{k}/\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{2},

where
e_{k}
is the unit vector.
Furthermore, invoking Lemma 5.3 in
47
, we know when we choose a suitable tuning parameter
\lambda_{k}\asymp K\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}
uniformly over
k
, we have
\max_{k}1/\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{2}=O_{p}(1)
. Hence,
\norm{\bI_p-\wh\bOmega\wh\bSigma_{n+N}}_{\max}\leq\max_{k}\left(\lambda_{k}/\widehat{\tau}_{k}^{2}\right)=O_{p}\left(K\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}\right)
.

In addition, recalling from the derivation of (S.17) in
18
, we have

\norm{\frac{\bX\trans(\bY- \fb_n)}{n}+\frac{\wt\bX\trans( \fb_{n+N}-\wt\bX\bt^*)}{n+N}}_{\infty}=O_{p}\left(K_{1}\sigma\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}+K_{1}\Phi\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}\right),

(S.16)

where
{\bf b}_{n}=\left\{f({\bf X}_{1}),\dots,f({\bf X}_{n})\right\}^{\rm T}
and
{\bf b}_{n+N}
is defined similarly.

Given the above preliminary results, we focus on deriving the limiting distribution of
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})
. Recall that we use the following notation
\widehat{\bf b}^{-j}_{D^{*}_{j}}=\{\widehat{f}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i}):i\in D_{j}^{*}\}
,
\widehat{\bf b}^{-j}_{D_{j}}=\{\widehat{f}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i}):i\in D_{j}\}
, and
{\bf b}_{D^{*}_{j}}
and
{\bf b}_{D_{j}}
are defined similarly. We decompose the term
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})
as

\displaystyle{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\left[(\mathbb{I}_{p}-\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{n+N})(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{SD}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})+\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\left\{\frac{\mathbb{X}_{D^{*}_{j}}^{\rm T}({\bf Y}_{D^{*}_{j}}-\widehat{\bf b}^{-j}_{D^{*}_{j}})}{2n_{j}}+\frac{\widetilde{\mathbb{X}}_{D_{j}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bf b}^{-j}_{D_{j}}-\widetilde{\mathbb{X}}_{D_{j}}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})}{2n_{j}+2N_{j}}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\left[(\mathbb{I}_{p}-\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{n+N})(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{SD}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})+(\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}-{\bm{\Omega}})\left\{\frac{\mathbb{X}^{\rm T}({\bf Y}-{\bf b}_{n})}{n}+\frac{\widetilde{\mathbb{X}}^{\rm T}({\bf b}_{n+N}-\widetilde{\mathbb{X}}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})}{n+N}\right\}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+{\bm{\Omega}}\left\{\frac{\mathbb{X}^{\rm T}({\bf Y}-{\bf b}_{n})}{n}+\frac{\widetilde{\mathbb{X}}^{\rm T}({\bf b}_{n+N}-\widetilde{\mathbb{X}}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})}{n+N}\right\}-{\bm{\Omega}}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\left\{\frac{\mathbb{X}_{D^{*}_{j}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bf b}^{-j}_{D^{*}_{j}}-{\bf b}_{D^{*}_{j}})}{2n_{j}}+\frac{\widetilde{\mathbb{X}}_{D_{j}}^{\rm T}({\bf b}_{D_{j}}-\widehat{\bf b}^{-j}_{D_{j}})}{2n_{j}+2N_{j}}\right\}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+({\bm{\Omega}}-\widehat{\bm{\Omega}})\sum_{j=1}^{2}\left\{\frac{\mathbb{X}_{D^{*}_{j}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bf b}^{-j}_{D^{*}_{j}}-{\bf b}_{D^{*}_{j}})}{2n_{j}}+\frac{\widetilde{\mathbb{X}}_{D_{j}}^{\rm T}({\bf b}_{D_{j}}-\widehat{\bf b}^{-j}_{D_{j}})}{2n_{j}+2N_{j}}\right\}\right].

(S.17)

Therefore, with the preliminary results above in hand, we can show that

\displaystyle\norm{(\bI_p-\wh\bOmega\wh\bSigma_{n+N})(\wh\bt_{SD}-\bt^*)}_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\norm{\bI_p-\wh\bOmega\wh\bSigma_{n+N}}_{\max}\norm{\wh\bt_{SD}-\bt^*}_{1}

\displaystyle=O_{p}\left[K_{1}Ks\left\{\Phi{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}+\sigma{\frac{\log p}{\sqrt{n(n+N)}}}+b_{n}{\frac{\log p}{\sqrt{n(n+N)}}}\right\}\right],

from (
S.15
) and Theorem 3.2 of
18
. Similarly,

\displaystyle\norm{ (\wh\bOmega-\bOmega)\left\{\frac{\bX\trans(\bY- \fb_{n})}{n}+\frac{\wt\bX\trans( \fb_{n+N}-\wt\bX\bt^*)}{n+N}\right\}}_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\norm{ \wh\bOmega-\bOmega}_{\infty}\norm{\frac{\bX\trans(\bY- \fb_{n})}{n}+\frac{\wt\bX\trans( \fb_{n+N}-\wt\bX\bt^*)}{n+N}}_{\infty}

\displaystyle=O_{p}\left[K_{1}Ks_{\Omega}\left\{\Phi{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}+\sigma{\frac{\log p}{\sqrt{n(n+N)}}}\right\}\right],

from (
S.16
) and (
S.15
). Following the similar argument in the analysis of
I_{1}
in (S.14) of
18
together with the assumption that
\norm{\bW}_{\infty}=\norm{\bOmega X}_{\infty}\leq K_{2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\norm{\bOmega\sum_{j=1}^2\left\{\frac{\bX_{D^*_j}\trans(\wh\fb^{-j}_{D^*_j}- \fb_{D^*_j})}{2n_j}+\frac{\wt\bX_{D_j}\trans( \fb_{D_j}-\wh\fb^{-j}_{D_j})}{2n_j+2N_j}\right\}}_{\infty}=O_{p}\left(K_{2}b_{n}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right).

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\norm{(\bOmega-\wh\bOmega) \sum_{j=1}^2\left\{\frac{\bX_{D^*_j}\trans(\wh\fb^{-j}_{D^*_j}- \fb_{D^*_j})}{2n_j}+\frac{\wt\bX_{D_j}\trans( \fb_{D_j}-\wh\fb^{-j}_{D_j})}{2n_j+2N_j}\right\}}_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\norm{\bOmega-\wh\bOmega}_{\infty}\norm{\sum_{j=1}^2\left\{\frac{\bX_{D^*_j}\trans(\wh\fb^{-j}_{D^*_j}- \fb_{D^*_j})}{2n_j}+\frac{\wt\bX_{D_j}\trans( \fb_{D_j}-\wh\fb^{-j}_{D_j})}{2n_j+2N_j}\right\}}_{\infty}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O_{p}\left\{K_{1}Kb_{n}s_{\Omega}\frac{\log p}{\sqrt{n(n+N)}}\right\}.

Collecting the above probability bounds and plugging into (
S.17
), we obtain

\displaystyle{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})

\displaystyle={\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}\left\{\frac{\mathbb{X}^{\rm T}({\bf Y}-{\bf b}_{n})}{n}+\frac{\widetilde{\mathbb{X}}^{\rm T}({\bf b}_{n+N}-\widetilde{\mathbb{X}}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})}{n+N}\right\}+O_{p}(\delta_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bf W}_{i}\{Y_{i}-f({\bf X}_{i})\}+\frac{1}{n+N}\sum_{i=1}^{n+N}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bf W}_{i}\{f({\bf X}_{i})-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\}+O_{p}(\delta_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n+N}\xi_{i}+O_{p}(\delta_{n}).

(S.18)

where

\xi_{i}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{n}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bf W}_{i}\left[Y_{i}-f({\bf X}_{i})+\frac{n}{n+N}\{f({\bf X}_{i})-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\}\right]&\mbox{for}~1\leq i\leq n,\\
\frac{1}{n+N}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bf W}_{i}\{f({\bf X}_{i})-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\}&\mbox{for}~n+1\leq i\leq n+N\end{cases}

and

\delta_{n}=\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}\left[K_{1}K(s\vee s_{\Omega})\left\{\Phi{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}+\sigma{\frac{\log p}{\sqrt{n(n+N)}}}+b_{n}{\frac{\log p}{\sqrt{n(n+N)}}}\right\}+K_{2}b_{n}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right].

In the following, we will apply the Lindeberg-Feller Central Limit Theorem to (
S.18
). First, we note that
{\hbox{E}}[{\bf W}_{i}\{Y_{i}-f({\bf X}_{i})\}]={\bf 0}
and
{\hbox{E}}[{\bf W}_{i}\{f({\bf X}_{i})-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\}]={\bf 0}
. Denote
\eta_{i}=f({\bf X}_{i})-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
. We have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n+N}{\hbox{E}}(\xi_{i}^{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{n^{2}}{\hbox{E}}\left\{{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bf W}_{i}\left(\epsilon_{i}+\frac{n}{n+N}\eta_{i}\right)\right\}^{2}+\sum_{i=n+1}^{n+N}\frac{1}{(n+N)^{2}}{\hbox{E}}({\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bf W}_{i}\eta_{i})^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\left(\sigma^{2}{\bm{\Omega}}+\frac{n}{n+N}{\bm{\Gamma}}\right){\bm{v}}:=t_{n}^{2},

where
{\bm{\Gamma}}={\rm{Cov}}[{\bf W}\{f({\bf X})-{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\}]
. The Lyapunov condition holds as follows

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n+N}{\hbox{E}}|\xi_{i}|^{2+\delta}}{t_{n}^{2+\delta}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2+\delta}K_{2}^{2+\delta}\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\hbox{E}}(\epsilon_{i}+\frac{n}{n+N}\eta_{i})^{2+\delta}/n^{2+\delta}+\sum_{i=n+1}^{n+N}{\hbox{E}}\eta_{i}^{2+\delta}/(n+N)^{2+\delta}\}}{t_{n}^{2+\delta}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2+\delta}K_{2}^{2+\delta}[2^{1+\delta}\{{\hbox{E}}\epsilon_{i}^{2+\delta}+(\frac{n}{n+N})^{2+\delta}{\hbox{E}}\eta_{i}^{2+\delta}\}/n^{1+\delta}+{\hbox{E}}\eta_{i}^{2+\delta}N/(n+N)^{2+\delta}]}{t_{n}^{2+\delta}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2^{1+\delta}\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2+\delta}K_{2}^{2+\delta}{\hbox{E}}\epsilon_{i}^{2+\delta}}{n^{\delta/2}(\sigma^{2}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}})^{1+\delta/2}}+\frac{\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2+\delta}K_{2}^{2+\delta}(2^{1+\delta}n+N){\hbox{E}}\eta_{i}^{2+\delta}}{(n+N)^{1+\delta/2}({\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Gamma}}{\bm{v}})^{1+\delta/2}}\rightarrow 0,

where the first inequality follows from
\norm{\bW}_{\infty}=\norm{\bOmega X}_{\infty}\leq K_{2}
and the second one is due to the convexity of the function
x^{2+\delta}
for
x>0
. Therefore, the Lindeberg-Feller Central Limit Theorem leads to

\sum_{i=1}^{n+N}\xi_{i}/t_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{N}(0,1).

From (
S.18
) we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})}{t_{n}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n+N}\xi_{i}/t_{n}+O_{p}(\delta_{n}/t_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{N}(0,1),

as
\delta_{n}/t_{n}=o(1)
. This completes the proof.

∎

S.3.4
Proof of Proposition
S.1

Lemma
S.4
.

Under the same conditions in Proposition
S.1
, we have

|\widehat{\sigma}^{2}-\sigma^{2}|=O_{p}(n^{-1/2}+b^{2}_{n}),

(S.19)

and

\Big|{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}-{\bm{\Gamma}}){\bm{v}}\Big|=O_{p}\left\{K^{2}\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2}\left(b_{n}+K\sqrt{\frac{s\log p}{n}}+\frac{K^{2}s\log p}{n}+Ks_{\Omega}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}\right)\right\}.

(S.20)

Proof of Lemma
S.4
.

To show (
S.19
), it suffices to upper bound
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{j}-\sigma^{2}
, that is

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{j}-\sigma^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D^{*}_{j}}\left\{Y_{i}-f({\bf X}_{i})+f({\bf X}_{i})-\widehat{f}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})\right\}^{2}-\sigma^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D^{*}_{j}}(\epsilon_{i}^{2}-\sigma^{2})+\frac{2}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D^{*}_{j}}\epsilon_{i}\left\{f({\bf X}_{i})-\widehat{f}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})\right\}+\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D^{*}_{j}}\left\{f({\bf X}_{i})-\widehat{f}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})\right\}^{2}.

(S.21)

Chebyshev’s inequality together with the assumption
{\hbox{E}}(\epsilon^{4})\leq C
implies
\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D^{*}_{j}}(\epsilon_{i}^{2}-\sigma^{2})=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
. As in the derivation of (S.16) in
18
we have

{\mathbb{P}}\left[\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D^{*}_{j}}\epsilon_{i}\left\{f({\bf X}_{i})-\widehat{f}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})\right\}>c\sigma b_{n}/n_{j}^{1/2}|D_{j}^{*}\right]\leq\frac{\|\widehat{f}^{-j}-f\|_{2}^{2}}{c^{2}b_{n}^{2}}\wedge 1.

As a result, we have

\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D^{*}_{j}}\epsilon_{i}\left\{f({\bf X}_{i})-\widehat{f}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})\right\}=O_{p}\left(\frac{b_{n}}{n^{1/2}}\right).

Similarly,
\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D^{*}_{j}}\{f({\bf X}_{i})-\widehat{f}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})\}^{2}\lesssim\|\widehat{f}^{-j}-f\|_{2}^{2}=O_{p}(b^{2}_{n})
. Plugging into (
S.21
), we have
|\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{j}-\sigma^{2}|=O_{p}(n^{-1/2}+b^{2}_{n})
, which further implies (
S.19
).

To show (
S.20
), we decompose
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{j}-{\bm{\Gamma}}){\bm{v}}
as follows

\displaystyle{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{j}-{\bm{\Gamma}}){\bm{v}}

\displaystyle=\hbox to168.3pt{\vbox to43.01pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 84.14839pt\lower-33.80551pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-5.85939pt}{-25.22244pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{68}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{

(S.22)

Let us first consider
T_{1}
, which can be rewritten as

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{i}+N_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}}({\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}{\bf X}_{i})^{2}\{\widehat{\eta}_{i}^{-j}-\eta_{i}\}^{2}+\frac{2}{n_{i}+N_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}}({\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}{\bf X}_{i})^{2}\eta_{i}\{\widehat{\eta}_{i}^{-j}-\eta_{i}\}.

We know that
|{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}{\bf X}_{i}|\leq\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}\|\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}{\bf X}_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}(\|{\bm{\Omega}}{\bf X}_{i}\|_{\infty}+\|\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}-{\bm{\Omega}}\|_{\infty}\|{\bf X}_{i}\|_{\infty})\lesssim K\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}
, since
Ks_{\Omega}\{\log p/(n+N)\}^{1/2}=o(1)
. In addition,
(\widehat{\eta}_{i}^{-j}-\eta_{i})^{2}\leq 2\{\widehat{f}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})-f({\bf X}_{i})\}^{2}+2\{{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{SD}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})\}^{2}
. Combining these results, Theorem 3.2 of
18
and
b_{n}=o(1)
, we derive

\Big|\frac{1}{n_{i}+N_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}}({\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}{\bf X}_{i})^{2}(\widehat{\eta}_{i}^{-j}-\eta_{i})^{2}\Big|=O_{p}\left\{K^{2}\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2}\left(b_{n}^{2}+\frac{K_{1}^{2}s\log p}{n}\right)\right\}

and

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{1}{n_{i}+N_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}}({\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}{\bf X}_{i})^{2}\eta_{i}(\widehat{\eta}_{i}^{-j}-\eta_{i})\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\Big|\frac{1}{n_{i}+N_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}}({\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}{\bf X}_{i})^{2}\eta_{i}^{2}\Big|^{1/2}\Big|\frac{1}{n_{i}+N_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}}({\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}{\bf X}_{i})^{2}(\widehat{\eta}_{i}^{-j}-\eta_{i})^{2}\Big|^{1/2}

\displaystyle=O_{p}\left\{K^{2}\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2}\left(b_{n}+K\sqrt{\frac{s\log p}{n}}\right)\right\},

where the first step holds by Cauchy–Schwarz inequality. This implies the following rate for
T_{1}
:

|T_{1}|=O_{p}\left\{K^{2}\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2}\left(b_{n}+K\sqrt{\frac{s\log p}{n}}+\frac{K^{2}s\log p}{n}\right)\right\}.

For
T_{2}
, we can show that

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle={\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}-{\bm{\Omega}}){\bf Z}_{n}(\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}-{\bm{\Omega}}){\bm{v}}+2{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}-{\bm{\Omega}}){\bf Z}_{n}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}+{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bf Z}_{n}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}

where
{\bf Z}_{n}=\frac{1}{n_{i}+N_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}}\{{\bf X}_{i}{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}\eta_{i}^{2}-{\hbox{E}}({\bf X}_{i}{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}\eta_{i}^{2})\}
. We can bound the three terms in the right hand side of the above equation separately. As an illustration, we have

{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}-{\bm{\Omega}}){\bf Z}_{n}(\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}-{\bm{\Omega}}){\bm{v}}\leq\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2}\|\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}-{\bm{\Omega}}\|^{2}_{\infty}\|{\bf Z}_{n}\|_{\max}=O_{p}\left(\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2}\frac{K^{2}s^{2}_{\Omega}\log p}{n+N}K^{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}\right),

where in the last step holds we plugin the rate of
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}
in (
S.15
) and apply Lemma
S.2
to upper bound
\|{\bf Z}_{n}\|_{\max}
(as
X_{i}
is uniformly bounded by
K_{1}\leq K
and
{\hbox{E}}(\eta_{i}^{2})
is bounded as well). Using a similar argument, one can derive

|{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}-{\bm{\Omega}}){\bf Z}_{n}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}|\leq\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}\|\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}-{\bm{\Omega}}\|_{\infty}\|{\bf Z}_{n}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}\|_{\infty}=O_{p}\left(\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2}Ks_{\Omega}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}K^{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}\right),

and

{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bf Z}_{n}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}=O_{p}\left(\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2}K^{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}\right).

Under the additional assumption that
Ks_{\Omega}\{\log p/(n+N)\}^{1/2}=o(1)
, we can simplify the rate of
T_{2}
as

|T_{2}|=O_{p}\left(\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2}K^{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}\right).

Finally, let us consider
T_{3}
and
T_{4}
. For
T_{3}
, we have

T_{3}={\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}-{\bm{\Omega}}){\hbox{E}}({\bf X}_{i}{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}\eta_{i}^{2}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}})+{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}-{\bm{\Omega}}){\hbox{E}}({\bf X}_{i}{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}\eta_{i}^{2})(\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}-{\bm{\Omega}}){\bm{v}},

where the first term is identical to
T_{4}
and therefore it suffices to only consider the rate of
T_{3}
. Since
\|{\hbox{E}}({\bf X}_{i}{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}\eta_{i}^{2}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}})\|_{\infty}\leq\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}K^{2}{\hbox{E}}(\eta_{i}^{2})\lesssim\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}K^{2}
and
\|{\hbox{E}}({\bf X}_{i}{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}\eta_{i}^{2})\|_{\max}\lesssim K^{2}
, we have

|T_{3}|\lesssim\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}Ks_{\Omega}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}K^{2}+\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2}\frac{K^{2}s^{2}_{\Omega}\log p}{n+N}K^{2}=O_{p}\left(\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2}K^{3}s_{\Omega}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}\right).

Collecting the upper bounds for
T_{1},\dots,T_{4}
, from (
S.22
) we obtain the rate in (
S.20
).
∎

Proof of Proposition
S.1
.

Note that
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}\leq\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}^{2}\lambda_{\max}({\bm{\Omega}})\lesssim\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}^{2}
since
\lambda_{\max}({\bm{\Omega}})=1/\lambda_{\min}({\bm{\Sigma}})\leq 1/C
. From Lemma
S.4
, we can show that

\displaystyle{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\sigma}^{2}\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}-{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\sigma^{2}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}

\displaystyle=(\widehat{\sigma}^{2}-\sigma^{2}){\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}+(\widehat{\sigma}^{2}-\sigma^{2}){\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}-{\bm{\Omega}}){\bm{v}}+\sigma^{2}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}-{\bm{\Omega}}){\bm{v}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}^{2}(n^{-1/2}+b_{n}^{2})+(n^{-1/2}+b_{n}^{2})\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}\|{\bm{v}}\|_{\infty}Ks_{\Omega}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}+\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}\|{\bm{v}}\|_{\infty}Ks_{\Omega}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}

\displaystyle=O_{p}\left\{\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}^{2}(n^{-1/2}+b_{n}^{2})+\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}\|{\bm{v}}\|_{\infty}Ks_{\Omega}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}\right\}.

This implies

\displaystyle\left|{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\left(\widehat{\sigma}^{2}\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}+\frac{n}{n+N}\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}\right){\bm{v}}-{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\left(\sigma^{2}{\bm{\Omega}}+\frac{n}{n+N}{\bm{\Gamma}}\right){\bm{v}}\right|

\displaystyle=O_{p}\left\{\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}^{2}(n^{-1/2}+b_{n}^{2})+\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}\|{\bm{v}}\|_{\infty}Ks_{\Omega}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.~~~~+K^{2}\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2}\left(\frac{nb_{n}}{n+N}+K\sqrt{\frac{n}{n+N}}\sqrt{\frac{s\log p}{n+N}}+\frac{K^{2}s\log p}{n+N}+Ks_{\Omega}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}\frac{n}{n+N}\right)\right\}.

By applying the condition
K\{s\log p/(n+N)\}^{1/2}=o(1)
and
n/(n+N)\leq 1
, we can further simplify the above rate and derive (
S.12
). Note that
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\sigma^{2}{\bm{\Omega}}+\frac{n}{n+N}{\bm{\Gamma}}){\bm{v}}\geq C\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}^{2}
which together with (
S.12
) leads to

\left|{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\left(\widehat{\sigma}^{2}\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}+\frac{n}{n+N}\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}\right){\bm{v}}/{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\left(\sigma^{2}{\bm{\Omega}}+\frac{n}{n+N}{\bm{\Gamma}}\right){\bm{v}}-1\right|=O_{p}(n^{-1/2}+b_{n}^{2}+Rem_{N}/\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}^{2}).

Finally, (
S.14
) holds by Theorem
S.1
and the Slutsky Theorem. This completes the proof.
∎

S.4
Proof of Theorem
1

We first state several propositions and lemmas which are used in the proof.

Lemma
S.5
.

Assume that Assumption
S.1
holds. Consider the Dantzig selector
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{D}
in (
2
) with
\lambda_{D}\asymp K_{1}\sqrt{\frac{(\sigma^{2}+\Phi^{2})\log p}{n}}
. We have

\norm{\wh\bt_D-\bt^*}_{1}=O_{p}(s\lambda_{D}),~~\textrm{and}~~\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{D}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})\}^{2}=O_{p}(s\lambda_{D}^{2}).

(S.23)

Moreover, we have

\|\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{n}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{D}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})\|_{\infty}=O_{p}(\lambda_{D}),

where
\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\bf X}_{i}^{\otimes 2}
.

Proof.

The proof of the convergence rate of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{D}
in (
S.23
) is similar to Theorem 7.1 in
45
. The key step is to derive

\norm{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \X_i(Y_i-\X_i\trans\bt^*)}_{\infty}\lesssim K_{1}(\sigma^{2}+\Phi^{2})^{1/2}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}},

which is implied by Lemma
S.2
together with
{\hbox{E}}(Y_{i}-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})^{2}=\sigma^{2}+\Phi^{2}
and
\|{\bf X}_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq K_{1}
. The rest of the proof is omitted. To show the rate of
\|\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{n}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{D}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})\|_{\infty}
, we note that, with
\lambda_{D}=CK_{1}\sqrt{\frac{(\sigma^{2}+\Phi^{2})\log p}{n}}
for some sufficiently large
C
, we have

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{n}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{D}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\bf X}_{i}(Y_{i}-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{D})\right\|_{\infty}+\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\bf X}_{i}(Y_{i}-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})\right\|_{\infty}\leq 2\bar{\lambda},

where we invoke the KKT condition of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{D}
in the last step.
∎

Proposition
S.2
.

Under the same conditions in Theorem
1
, for any
\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}=\widetilde{\lambda}\geq CK_{1}(c_{n}+\sqrt{\log p/n})
, we obtain that

\norm{\wh\bB_{\cdot k}- \bB_{\cdot k}}_{1}\lesssim s_{B}\widetilde{\lambda}+\widetilde{\lambda}^{-1}\left\{K_{1}L_{B}\left(c_{n}+\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right)+K_{1}^{2}\sqrt{\frac{s(\sigma^{2}+\Phi^{2})\log p}{n}}\right\}^{2}.

Define

\widetilde{\lambda}_{opt}=\argmin_{\widetilde{\lambda}\geq CK_{1}(c_{n}+\sqrt{\log p/n})}s_{B}\widetilde{\lambda}+\widetilde{\lambda}^{-1}\left\{K_{1}L_{B}\left(c_{n}+\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right)+K_{1}^{2}\sqrt{\frac{s(\sigma^{2}+\Phi^{2})\log p}{n}}\right\}^{2}.

By choosing
\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}=\widetilde{\lambda}_{opt}
, we obtain

\norm{\wh\bB_{\cdot k}- \bB_{\cdot k}}_{1}\lesssim K_{1}(s_{B}+s_{B}^{1/2}L_{B})\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}+c_{n}\right)+K_{1}^{2}\sqrt{\frac{ss_{B}(\sigma^{2}+\Phi^{2})\log p}{n}},

which holds uniformly over
1\leq k\leq p

Proof.

The proof is deferred to the Supplement
S.4.2
.
∎

Now we are ready to prove Theorem
1
. For notational simplicity, we use
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S}
for
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi}
,
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}_{S}
for
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}_{S,\psi}
and
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}
for
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{D}
. We can rewrite

\displaystyle{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})

\displaystyle={\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\{\widehat{\bm{\theta}}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*}-\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{n}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})+\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}(\widehat{\bm{\xi}}_{S}-\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{n}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})\}

\displaystyle={\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\Big\{(\mathbb{I}_{p}-\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{n})(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})+{\bf J}\Big\}

\displaystyle={\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\Big\{(\mathbb{I}_{p}-{\bm{\Omega}}\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{n})(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})+({\bm{\Omega}}-\widehat{\bm{\Omega}})\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{n}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})+{\bf J}\Big\},

(S.24)

where

{\bf J}=\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}\left[\frac{\mathbb{X}^{\rm T}({\bf Y}-\mathbb{X}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})}{n}-\frac{\psi}{2}\widehat{\mathbb{B}}^{\rm T}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\left\{\frac{\sum_{i\in D^{*}_{j}}{\bf X}_{i}\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})}{n_{j}}-\frac{\sum_{i\in D_{j}}{\bf X}_{i}\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})}{n_{j}+N_{j}}\right\}\right].

We notice that
\|{\bm{\Omega}}{\bf X}_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq\|{\bm{\Omega}}\|_{\infty}\|{\bf X}_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq L_{\Omega}K_{1}
, applying Hoeffding inequality,

\norm{\bI_p-\bOmega\wh\bSigma_n}_{\max}=O_{p}\left(K_{1}^{2}L_{\Omega}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right)
.
Hence,

\norm{(\bI_p-\bOmega\wh\bSigma_n)(\wh\bt-\bt^*)}_{\infty}\leq\norm{\bI_p-\bOmega\wh\bSigma_n}_{\max}\norm{\wh\bt-\bt^*}_{1}\lesssim K_{1}^{3}L_{\Omega}(\sigma^{2}+\Phi^{2})^{1/2}\frac{s\log p}{n},

(S.25)

where we use the convergence rate of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}
in Lemma
S.5
. Moreover, we know

\norm{(\bOmega-\wh\bOmega)\wh\bSigma_n(\wh\bt-\bt^*)}_{\infty}\leq\norm{\bOmega-\wh\bOmega}_{\infty}\norm{\wh\bSigma_n(\wh\bt-\bt^*)}_{\infty}\lesssim K_{1}^{2}L_{\Omega}s_{\Omega}\frac{(\sigma^{2}+\Phi^{2})^{1/2}\log p}{\sqrt{(n+N)n}},

(S.26)

followed by (
S.15
) (we replace
K
with
K_{1}L_{\Omega}
) and again Lemma
S.5
. Now, we focus on the term
{\bf J}
. We rewrite
{\bf J}
as

\displaystyle{\bf J}

\displaystyle={\bm{\Omega}}\left[\frac{\mathbb{X}^{\rm T}({\bf Y}-\mathbb{X}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})}{n}-\psi\mathbb{B}^{\rm T}\left\{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\bf X}_{i}m({\bf X}_{i})}{n}-\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n+N}{\bf X}_{i}m({\bf X}_{i})}{n+N}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle+\hbox to252.65pt{\vbox to40.04pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 126.3222pt\lower-32.3333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
