}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}
\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -27.69 -11.35 M -27.69 -11.35 C -26.65 -13.42 -24.23 -14.81 -20.77 -14.81 L -6.92 -14.81 C -3.46 -14.81 -1.04 -16.19 0 -18.26 C 1.04 -16.19 3.46 -14.81 6.92 -14.81 L 20.77 -14.81 C 24.23 -14.81 26.65 -13.42 27.69 -11.35}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-5.21527pt}{-24.79411pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{68}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

where

\bm{\xi}_{0}=\frac{\mathbb{X}^{\rm T}{\bf Y}}{n}-\psi\mathbb{B}^{\rm T}\left\{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\bf X}_{i}m({\bf X}_{i})}{n}-\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n+N}{\bf X}_{i}m({\bf X}_{i})}{n+N}\right\}.

In the following, we will show that the three terms
\|{\bf J}_{k}\|_{\infty}
for
k=1,2,3
are sufficiently small. We first recall that

\norm{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \X_im(\X_i)-\bmu}_{\infty}\lesssim K_{1}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}},~~~\norm{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \X_i(Y_i-\X_i\trans\bt^*)}_{\infty}\lesssim K_{1}(\sigma^{2}+\Phi^{2})^{1/2}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}},

by Hoeffding inequality and Lemma
S.2
. For
{\bf J}_{1}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|{\bf J}_{1}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\norm{\wh\bOmega-\bOmega}_{\infty}\norm{\frac{\bX\trans(\bY-\bX\bt^*)}{n}-\psi\bB\trans\left\{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n} \X_i m(\X_i)}{n}-\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n+N} \X_i m(\X_i)}{n+N}\right\}}_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\norm{\wh\bOmega-\bOmega}_{\infty}\left\{\norm{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \X_i(Y_i-\X_i\trans\bt^*)}_{\infty}+|\psi|L_{B}\norm{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \X_im(\X_i)-\bmu}_{\infty}\right.

\displaystyle\left.~~~~~~~+|\psi|L_{B}\norm{\frac{1}{n+N}\sum_{i=1}^{n+N} \X_im(\X_i)-\bmu}_{\infty}\right\}

\displaystyle\lesssim K_{1}^{2}L_{\Omega}\left\{(\sigma^{2}+\Phi^{2})^{1/2}+L_{B}\right\}s_{\Omega}\frac{\log p}{\sqrt{(n+N)n}}.

(S.27)

To upper bound the supnorm of
{\bf J}_{2}
and
{\bf J}_{3}
, we need the following bounds. First, by Proposition
S.2
, we have

\displaystyle\norm{(\wh\bB- \bB)\trans\left\{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n} \X_i m(\X_i)}{n}-\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n+N} \X_i m(\X_i)}{n+N}\right\}}_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{k}\norm{\wh\bB_k- \bB_k}_{1}\left\{\norm{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \X_im(\X_i)-\bmu}_{\infty}+\norm{\frac{1}{n+N}\sum_{i=1}^{n+N} \X_im(\X_i)-\bmu}_{\infty}\right\}

\displaystyle\lesssim K_{1}^{2}(s_{B}+s_{B}^{1/2}L_{B})\left(\frac{\log p}{n}+c_{n}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right)+K_{1}^{3}{\frac{\sqrt{ss_{B}(\sigma^{2}+\Phi^{2})}\log p}{n}}.

Furthermore, using a similar argument in the proof of Theorem 3.2 of
18
, we have

\displaystyle\norm{ \bB\trans\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^2\left[\frac{\sum_{i\in D^*_{j}} \X_i \left\{\wh m^{-j}(\X_i)-m(\X_i)\right\}}{n_j}-\frac{\sum_{i\in D_j}\X_i \left\{\wh m^{-j}(\X_i)-m(\X_i)\right\}}{n_j+N_j}\right]}_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq L_{B}\max_{j=1,2}\norm{\frac{\sum_{i\in D^*_{j}} \X_i \left\{\wh m^{-j}(\X_i)-m(\X_i)\right\}}{n_j}-\frac{\sum_{i\in D_j}\X_i \left\{\wh m^{-j}(\X_i)-m(\X_i)\right\}}{n_j+N_j}}_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim K_{1}L_{B}c_{n}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}.

Under the condition
s_{B}K_{1}^{2}\left(c_{n}+\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right)=o(1)
, one can easily verify that

\max_{k}\norm{\wh\bB_k- \bB_k}_{1}\lesssim L_{B}+K_{1}^{2}\sqrt{\frac{ss_{B}(\sigma^{2}+\Phi^{2})\log p}{n}}
, and therefore,

\displaystyle\norm{(\wh\bB- \bB)\trans\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^2\left[\frac{\sum_{i\in D^*_{j}} \X_i \left\{\wh m^{-j}(\X_i)-m(\X_i)\right\}}{n_j}-\frac{\sum_{i\in D_j}\X_i \left\{\wh m^{-j}(\X_i)-m(\X_i)\right\}}{n_j+N_j}\right]}_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim K_{1}L_{B}c_{n}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}+c_{n}K_{1}^{3}{\frac{\sqrt{ss_{B}(\sigma^{2}+\Phi^{2})}\log p}{n}},

which is of smaller order than the previous two terms. Given these bounds, we can decompose and bound
\norm{\bxi_0- \wh\bxi_S}_{\infty}
as

\displaystyle\norm{\bxi_0- \wh\bxi_S}_{\infty}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\psi|\norm{\frac{1}{2}\wh\bB\trans\sum_{j=1}^2\left\{\frac{\sum_{i\in D^*_{j}}\X_i \wh m^{-j}(X_i)}{n_j}-\frac{\sum_{i\in D_j} \X_i \wh m^{-j}(\X_i)}{n_j+N_j}\right\}
-\bB\trans\left\{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n} \X_i m(\X_i)}{n}-\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n+N} \X_i m(\X_i)}{n+N}\right\}}_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\norm{(\wh\bB- \bB)\trans\left\{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n} \X_i m(\X_i)}{n}-\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{n+N} \X_i m(\X_i)}{n+N}\right\}}_{\infty}

\displaystyle~~~+\norm{(\wh\bB- \bB)\trans\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^2\left[\frac{\sum_{i\in D^*_{j}}\X_i \left\{\wh m^{-j}(\X_i)-m(\X_i)\right\}}{n_j}-\frac{\sum_{i\in D_j} \X_i \left\{\wh m^{-j}(\X_i)-m(\X_i)\right\}}{n_j+N_j}\right]}_{\infty}

\displaystyle~~~+\norm{\bB\trans\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^2\left[\frac{\sum_{i\in D^*_{j}} \X_i \left\{\wh m^{-j}(\X_i)-m(\X_i)\right\}}{n_j}-\frac{\sum_{i\in D_j}\X_i \left\{\wh m^{-j}(\X_i)-m(\X_i)\right\}}{n_j+N_j}\right]}_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim K_{1}^{2}(s_{B}+s_{B}^{1/2}L_{B})\left(\frac{\log p}{n}+c_{n}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right)+K_{1}^{3}{\frac{\sqrt{ss_{B}(\sigma^{2}+\Phi^{2})}\log p}{n}}.

Thus, for
{\bf J}_{2}
and
{\bf J}_{3}
we have

\displaystyle\|J_{3}\|_{\infty}\lesssim L_{\Omega}\left\{K_{1}^{2}(s_{B}+s_{B}^{1/2}L_{B})\left(\frac{\log p}{n}+c_{n}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right)+K_{1}^{3}{\frac{\sqrt{ss_{B}(\sigma^{2}+\Phi^{2})}\log p}{n}}\right\}.

(S.28)

Since we have
K_{1}L_{\Omega}s_{\Omega}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}=o(1)
,
\|{\bf J}_{2}\|_{\infty}
is of smaller order than that of
\|{\bf J}_{3}\|_{\infty}
. Thus, from (
S.4
) and (
S.28
), we have

{\bf J}={\bm{\Omega}}\left[\frac{\mathbb{X}^{\rm T}({\bf Y}-\mathbb{X}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})}{n}-\psi\mathbb{B}^{\rm T}\left\{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\bf X}_{i}m({\bf X}_{i})}{n}-\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n+N}{\bf X}_{i}m({\bf X}_{i})}{n+N}\right\}\right]+Rem,

where

\displaystyle\|Rem\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim L_{\Omega}K_{1}^{2}\left[\{(\sigma^{2}+\Phi^{2})^{1/2}+L_{B}\}s_{\Omega}\frac{\log p}{\sqrt{(n+N)n}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.~~~~~+(s_{B}+s_{B}^{1/2}L_{B})\left(\frac{\log p}{n}+c_{n}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right)+K_{1}{\frac{\sqrt{ss_{B}(\sigma^{2}+\Phi^{2})}\log p}{n}}\right].

Finally, combining with (
S.25
) and (
S.26
), we obtain from (
S.24
) that

\displaystyle{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}({\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})

\displaystyle={\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}\left[\frac{\mathbb{X}^{\rm T}({\bf Y}-\mathbb{X}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})}{n}-\psi\mathbb{B}^{\rm T}\left\{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\bf X}_{i}m({\bf X}_{i})}{n}-\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n+N}{\bf X}_{i}m({\bf X}_{i})}{n+N}\right\}\right]+O_{p}(\bar{\delta}_{n}),

(S.29)

where

\displaystyle\bar{\delta}_{n}

\displaystyle=\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}L_{\Omega}K_{1}^{2}\left[\left\{(\sigma^{2}+\Phi^{2})^{1/2}+L_{B}\right\}s_{\Omega}\frac{\log p}{\sqrt{(n+N)n}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.~~~~~~~~~~~+(s_{B}+s_{B}^{1/2}L_{B})\left(\frac{\log p}{n}+c_{n}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right)+K_{1}(s\vee s_{B}){\frac{\sqrt{(\sigma^{2}+\Phi^{2})}\log p}{n}}\right].

To show the asymptotic normality of
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}({\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})
, we denote

\xi_{i}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{n}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}({\bf T}_{i1}-\frac{N\psi}{n+N}\mathbb{B}^{\rm T}{\bf T}_{i2})&\mbox{for}~1\leq i\leq n,\\
\frac{\psi}{n+N}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}\mathbb{B}^{\rm T}{\bf T}_{i2}&\mbox{for}~n+1\leq i\leq n+N,\end{cases}

where
{\bf T}_{i1}={\bf X}_{i}(Y_{i}-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})
and
{\bf T}_{i2}={\bf X}_{i}m({\bf X}_{i})-{\bm{\mu}}
.
One can rewrite (
S.29
) as

{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}({\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})=\sum_{i=1}^{n+N}\xi_{i}+O_{p}(\bar{\delta}_{n}).

To apply the Lindeberg-Feller Central Limit Theorem, we first note that
{\hbox{E}}(\xi_{i})=0
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n+N}{\hbox{E}}(\xi_{i}^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}\left\{{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i1}^{\otimes 2})-\frac{2N\psi}{n+N}\mathbb{B}^{\rm T}{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}{\bf T}_{i1}^{\rm T})+\frac{N^{2}\psi^{2}}{(n+N)^{2}}\mathbb{B}^{\rm T}{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}^{\otimes 2})\mathbb{B}\right\}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~+\frac{\psi^{2}N}{(n+N)^{2}}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}\mathbb{B}^{\rm T}{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}^{\otimes 2})\mathbb{B}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}\left\{{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i1}^{\otimes 2})-\frac{2N\psi}{n+N}\mathbb{B}^{\rm T}{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}{\bf T}_{i1}^{\rm T})+\frac{N\psi^{2}}{n+N}\mathbb{B}^{\rm T}{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}^{\otimes 2})\mathbb{B}\right\}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}.

Recall that
\mathbb{B}=\{{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}^{\otimes 2})\}^{-1}{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}{\bf T}_{i1}^{\rm T})
. Thus, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{n+N}{\hbox{E}}(\xi_{i}^{2})=\frac{1}{n}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}\left[{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i1}^{\otimes 2})-\frac{N(2\psi-\psi^{2})}{n+N}{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}{\bf T}_{i1}^{\rm T})^{\rm T}\{{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}^{\otimes 2})\}^{-1}{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}{\bf T}_{i1}^{\rm T})\right]{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}:=t_{n}^{2}.

Note that
{\hbox{E}}\|{\bf T}_{i1}\|_{\infty}^{2+\delta}\leq K_{1}^{2+\delta}{\hbox{E}}|\epsilon_{i}+\eta_{i}|^{2+\delta}\lesssim K_{1}^{2+\delta}
and
{\hbox{E}}\|{\bf T}_{i2}\|_{\infty}^{2+\delta}\lesssim K_{1}^{2+\delta}
. In addition, our assumption implies
t_{n}^{2+\delta}\geq C\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}^{2+\delta}/n^{1+\delta/2}
. Finally, we can verify that the Lyapunov condition holds

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n+N}{\hbox{E}}|\xi_{i}|^{2+\delta}}{t_{n}^{2+\delta}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2+\delta}L_{\Omega}^{2+\delta}}{t_{n}^{2+\delta}}\left\{\frac{1}{n^{1+\delta}}{\hbox{E}}\left\|{\bf T}_{i1}-\frac{N\psi}{n+N}\mathbb{B}^{\rm T}{\bf T}_{i2}\right\|^{2+\delta}_{\infty}+\frac{\psi^{2+\delta}N}{(n+N)^{2+\delta}}{\hbox{E}}\left\|\mathbb{B}^{\rm T}{\bf T}_{i2}\right\|_{\infty}^{2+\delta}\right\}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}L_{\Omega}K_{1}}{\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}}\right)^{2+\delta}\frac{1}{n^{\delta/2}}\left(1+\frac{L_{B}^{2+\delta}N}{n+N}\right)\rightarrow 0.

Therefore, we obtain the desired result by applying the Lindeberg-Feller Central Limit Theorem and Slutsky Theorem. This completes the proof.

S.4.1
Proof of Proposition
1

Proof.

We first establish the rate of
\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
and
\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
in the elementwise supnorm. Define
\mathbb{M}_{1}={\hbox{E}}\{(Y_{i}-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})^{2}{\bf X}_{i}^{\otimes 2}\}
. We note that

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{1}-\mathbb{M}_{1}\|_{\max}

\displaystyle\leq\Big\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_{i}-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})^{2}{\bf X}_{i}^{\otimes 2}-\mathbb{M}_{1}\Big\|_{\max}+\Big\|\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_{i}-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})\{{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{D}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})\}{\bf X}_{i}^{\otimes 2}\Big\|_{\max}

\displaystyle~~~~~+\Big\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{D}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})\}^{2}{\bf X}_{i}^{\otimes 2}\Big\|_{\max}\lesssim K_{1}^{3}\sqrt{\frac{s\log p}{n}},

where we use the moment inequality in Lemma
S.2
and Lemma
S.5
. Similarly, define
\mathbb{M}_{2}={\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}{\bf T}_{i1}^{\rm T})
. Since
\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
is constructed by sample splitting, it suffices to derive the rate of
\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}^{j}-\mathbb{M}_{2}
. Following the same type of argument, we can show that

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}^{j}-\mathbb{M}_{2}\|_{\max}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Big\|\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}(Y_{i}-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{D})\{\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})-m({\bf X}_{i})\}{\bf X}_{i}^{\otimes 2}\Big\|_{\max}+\Big\|\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{D}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})m({\bf X}_{i}){\bf X}_{i}^{\otimes 2}\Big\|_{\max}

\displaystyle~~~~~~+\Big\|\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}(Y_{i}-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})m({\bf X}_{i}){\bf X}_{i}^{\otimes 2}-\mathbb{M}_{2}\Big\|_{\max}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle K_{1}^{2}c_{n}+K_{1}^{3}\sqrt{\frac{s\log p}{n}}.

Together with Proposition
S.2
, we have

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\mathbb{B}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}-\mathbb{B}^{\rm T}\mathbb{M}_{2}\|_{\max}

\displaystyle\leq\|\widehat{\mathbb{B}}-\mathbb{B}\|_{\infty}\|\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}-\mathbb{M}_{2}\|_{\max}+\|\widehat{\mathbb{B}}-\mathbb{B}\|_{\infty}\|\mathbb{M}_{2}\|_{\max}+\|\mathbb{B}\|_{\infty}\|\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{2}-\mathbb{M}_{2}\|_{\max}

\displaystyle\lesssim K_{1}^{2}Rem,

where

Rem=K_{1}(s_{B}+s_{B}^{1/2}L_{B})\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}+c_{n}\right)+K_{1}^{2}\sqrt{\frac{ss_{B}\log p}{n}}+K_{1}L_{B}\sqrt{\frac{s\log p}{n}}

As a result,

\|\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}-{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}\|_{\max}\lesssim K_{1}^{3}\sqrt{\frac{s\log p}{n}}+\frac{NK_{1}^{2}}{n+N}Rem.

One can easily show that
\|{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}\|_{\max}\lesssim K_{1}^{2}+\frac{N}{n+N}L_{B}K_{1}^{2}
, which implies

\|\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}\|_{\max}\leq\|{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}\|_{\max}+\|\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}-{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}\|_{\max}\lesssim K_{1}^{2}+\frac{N}{n+N}L_{B}K_{1}^{2}

under the assumption that
Rem=o(1)
and
K_{1}\sqrt{\frac{s\log p}{n}}=o(1)
. Similarly,
\|\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}\|_{\infty}\lesssim L_{\Omega}
since
\|\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}-{\bm{\Omega}}\|_{\infty}=o(1)
. Finally, we can establish the rate of convergence of the estimated variance

\displaystyle|{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}-{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}|

\displaystyle\leq|{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}-{\bm{\Omega}})\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}|+|{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}(\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}-{\bm{\Omega}}){\bm{v}}|+|{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}(\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}-{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}){\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2}(L_{\Omega}\|\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}-{\bm{\Omega}}\|_{\infty}\|\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}\|_{\max}+L_{\Omega}^{2}\|\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}-{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}\|_{\max})

\displaystyle\lesssim\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2}\left(K_{1}L_{\Omega}^{2}s_{\Omega}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n+N}}\|{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}\|_{\max}+K_{1}^{3}L_{\Omega}^{2}\sqrt{\frac{s\log p}{n}}+\frac{NK_{1}^{2}L_{\Omega}^{2}}{n+N}Rem\right),

which proves (
15
). The proof of (
16
) is immediate by the Slutsky Theorem.
∎

S.4.2
Proof of Proposition
S.2

Proof.

To show Proposition
S.2
, it suffices to show that the same rate of convergence holds for
\widehat{\mathbb{B}}^{j}_{\cdot k}
. For simplicity of presentation, we use the notation
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{D}=\widehat{\bm{\theta}}
,
\widehat{\mathbb{B}}_{\cdot k}=\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
,
\mathbb{B}_{\cdot k}={\bm{\beta}}
,
\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}=\widehat{\bm{\beta}}-{\bm{\beta}}
and
\lambda=\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}
,
\widehat{\bf Z}_{i}={\bf X}_{i}\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})-\widehat{\bm{\mu}}^{j}
and
{\bf Z}_{i}={\bf X}_{i}m({\bf X}_{i})-{\bm{\mu}}
,

\widehat{\mathbb{F}}=\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}\widehat{\bf Z}_{i}\widehat{\bf Z}_{i}^{\rm T}
We start from the inequality

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}\left[X_{ik}(Y_{i}-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}})-\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}\left\{{\bf X}_{i}\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})-\widehat{\bm{\mu}}^{j}\right\}\right]^{2}+\lambda\norm{\wh\bbeta}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}\left[X_{ik}(Y_{i}-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}})-{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}\left\{{\bf X}_{i}\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})-\widehat{\bm{\mu}}^{j}\right\}\right]^{2}+\lambda\norm{\bbeta}_{1}.

Following the standard argument in the analysis of Lasso (e.g., the proof of Theorem 7.1 in
45
), the above inequality reduces to

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\mathbb{F}}\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda\norm{\bbeta}_{1}-\lambda\norm{\wh\bbeta}_{1}+\frac{2}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}\left\{X_{ik}(Y_{i}-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\theta}})-{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bf Z}_{i}\right\}\widehat{\bf Z}_{i}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}

(S.30)

\displaystyle=\lambda\norm{\bbeta}_{1}-\lambda\norm{\wh\bbeta}_{1}+I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}+I_{4},

where

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}\left\{X_{ik}(Y_{i}-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})-{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf Z}_{i}\right\}{\bf Z}_{i}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\Delta}},

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}\left\{X_{ik}(Y_{i}-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})-{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf Z}_{i}\right\}(\widehat{\bf Z}_{i}-{\bf Z}_{i})^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\Delta}},

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}X_{ik}{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})\widehat{\bf Z}_{i}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\Delta}},~~\textrm{and}~~~I_{4}=-\frac{2}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bf Z}_{i}-{\bf Z}_{i})\widehat{\bf Z}_{i}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}.

To bound
I_{1}
, we note that
{\hbox{E}}\left[\{X_{ik}(Y_{i}-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})-{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf Z}_{i}\}{\bf Z}_{i}\right]={\bf 0}
by the definition of
{\bm{\beta}}
. To invoke Lemma
S.2
, we control the second moment as

\displaystyle{\hbox{E}}\Big[\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\{X_{ik}(Y_{i}-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})-{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf Z}_{i}\}^{2}Z_{ij}^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\lesssim K_{1}^{2}{\hbox{E}}\Big[\{X_{ik}(Y_{i}-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})-{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf Z}_{i}\}^{2}\Big]\lesssim K_{1}^{2},

where first step
|Z_{ij}|=|X_{ij}m({\bf X}_{i})-\mu_{ij}|\lesssim K_{1}
holds as
|X_{ij}|\leq K_{1}
and
|m({\bf X}_{i})|\leq C
and second step relies on the assumption that the second moment of
X_{ik}(Y_{i}-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})-{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf Z}_{i}
is bounded. Therefore, applying Holder inequality and Lemma
S.2
with
m=2
, we have

\displaystyle|I_{1}|\lesssim\Big\|\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}\{X_{ik}(Y_{i}-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})-{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf Z}_{i}\}{\bf Z}_{i}\Big\|_{\infty}\|\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}\|_{1}\lesssim K_{1}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\|\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}\|_{1}.

(S.31)

Similarly, we have
|I_{2}|\lesssim\|\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}{\bf E}_{ik}(\widehat{\bf Z}_{i}-{\bf Z}_{i})\|_{\infty}\|\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}\|_{1}
, where
{\bf E}_{ik}=X_{ik}(Y_{i}-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})-{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf Z}_{i}
. Furthermore, we notice that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}{\bf E}_{ik}(\widehat{\bf Z}_{i}-{\bf Z}_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}{\bf E}_{ik}{\bf X}_{i}\left\{\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})-m({\bf X}_{i})\right\}

\displaystyle~~~-\left(\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}{\bf E}_{ik}\right)\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}\Big[{\bf X}_{i}\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})-{\hbox{E}}\{{\bf X}_{i}m({\bf X}_{i})\}\Big].

For the first term, we can apply Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to show that

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq l\leq p}\Big|\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}{\bf E}_{ik}X_{il}\{\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})-m({\bf X}_{i})\}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{1\leq l\leq p}\Big|\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}{\bf E}^{2}_{ik}X^{2}_{il}\Big|^{1/2}\Big|\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}\{\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})-m({\bf X}_{i})\}^{2}\Big|^{1/2}\lesssim K_{1}c_{n},

(S.32)

where in the last step we invoke Lemma
S.2
again to show

\max_{1\leq l\leq p}|\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}{\bf E}^{2}_{ik}X^{2}_{il}-{\hbox{E}}({\bf E}^{2}_{ik}X^{2}_{il})|\lesssim K_{1}^{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}
and
{\hbox{E}}({\bf E}^{2}_{ik}X^{2}_{il})\lesssim K_{1}^{2}
together with triangle inequality imply the desired bound. For the second term, we first notice that
\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}{\bf E}_{ik}=O_{p}(1)
, and then a similar argument leads to

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq l\leq p}\left|\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}\left[X_{il}\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})-{\hbox{E}}\left\{X_{il}m({\bf X}_{i})\right\}\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{1\leq l\leq p}\Big|\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}X_{il}\left\{\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})-m({\bf X}_{i})\right\}\Big|+\max_{1\leq l\leq p}\Big|\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}[X_{il}m({\bf X}_{i})-{\hbox{E}}\{X_{il}m({\bf X}_{i})\}]\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim K_{1}c_{n}+K_{1}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}},

(S.33)

where we apply the Hoeffding inequality as
|X_{il}m({\bf X}_{i})|\leq CK_{1}
. Combining (
S.4.2
) and (
S.4.2
), we have

\displaystyle|I_{2}|\lesssim\Big\|\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}{\bf E}_{ik}(\widehat{\bf Z}_{i}-{\bf Z}_{i})\Big\|_{\infty}\|\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}\|_{1}\lesssim\left(K_{1}c_{n}+K_{1}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right)\|\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}\|_{1}.

(S.34)

We now consider
I_{3}
. Recall that Lemma
S.5
implies
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})\right\}^{2}=O_{p}\left\{K_{1}^{2}s\frac{(\sigma^{2}+\Phi^{2})\log p}{n}\right\}
. Thus,

\displaystyle|I_{3}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big|\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}\{{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})\}^{2}{\bf X}^{2}_{ik}\Big|^{1/2}(\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\mathbb{F}}\widehat{\bm{\Delta}})^{1/2}\lesssim K_{1}^{2}\sqrt{\frac{s(\sigma^{2}+\Phi^{2})\log p}{n}}(\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\mathbb{F}}\widehat{\bm{\Delta}})^{1/2}.

(S.35)

Similarly, the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality yields
|I_{4}|\lesssim\left[\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}\left\{{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bf Z}_{i}-{\bf Z}_{i})\right\}^{2}\right]^{1/2}(\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\mathbb{F}}\widehat{\bm{\Delta}})^{1/2}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}\{{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bf Z}_{i}-{\bf Z}_{i})\}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i})^{2}\{\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})-m({\bf X}_{i})\}^{2}

\displaystyle~~~+2\left(\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}\left[{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i}m({\bf X}_{i})-{\hbox{E}}\{{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i}m({\bf X}_{i})\}\right]\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim K_{1}^{2}L_{B}^{2}c_{n}^{2}+\left(K_{1}L_{B}c_{n}+K_{1}L_{B}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right)^{2}

where the last step holds by using a similar argument as in (
S.4.2
) and (
S.4.2
) together with the fact that
|{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i}|\leq\|{\bm{\beta}}\|_{1}\|{\bf X}_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq K_{1}L_{B}
. As a result, we have

|I_{4}|\lesssim\left(K_{1}L_{B}c_{n}+K_{1}L_{B}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right)(\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\mathbb{F}}\widehat{\bm{\Delta}})^{1/2}.

Collecting the bounds in (
S.31
), (
S.34
) and (
S.35
), we obtain from (
S.30
) that

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\mathbb{F}}\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda\norm{\bbeta}_{1}-\lambda\norm{\wh\bbeta}_{1}+t_{1}\|\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}\|_{1}+t_{2}(\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\mathbb{F}}\widehat{\bm{\Delta}})^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda\norm{\wh\bDelta_S}_{1}-\lambda\norm{\wh\bDelta_{S^c}}_{1}+t_{1}\|\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}\|_{1}+t_{2}(\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\mathbb{F}}\widehat{\bm{\Delta}})^{1/2},

where
t_{1}=C\left(K_{1}c_{n}+K_{1}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right)
and
t_{2}=C\left\{K_{1}L_{B}\left(c_{n}+\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right)+K_{1}^{2}\sqrt{\frac{s(\sigma^{2}+\Phi^{2})\log p}{n}}\right\}
for some sufficiently large constant
C
. By taking
\lambda\geq 2t_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\mathbb{F}}\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}\leq\frac{3}{2}\lambda\norm{\wh\bDelta_S}_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\lambda\norm{\wh\bDelta_{S^c}}_{1}+t_{2}(\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\mathbb{F}}\widehat{\bm{\Delta}})^{1/2}.

(S.36)

In the following, we consider two cases. In case (1):
t_{2}(\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\mathbb{F}}\widehat{\bm{\Delta}})^{1/2}\leq\lambda\norm{\wh\bDelta_S}_{1}
, (
S.36
) further implies

\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\mathbb{F}}\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}\leq\frac{5}{2}\lambda\norm{\wh\bDelta_S}_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\lambda\norm{\wh\bDelta_{S^c}}_{1},

which leads to the standard cone condition
\norm{\wh\bDelta_{S^c}}_{1}\leq 5\norm{\wh\bDelta_S}_{1}
. With lemma
S.6
, we can show that
\|\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\lambda\norm{\wh\bDelta_S}_{1}\leq\lambda s_{B}^{1/2}\norm{\wh\bDelta_S}_{2}
and therefore
\|\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}\|_{2}\lesssim\lambda s_{B}^{1/2}
. Similarly, we can derive
\|\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}\|_{1}\lesssim\lambda s_{B}
. In case (2):
t_{2}(\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\mathbb{F}}\widehat{\bm{\Delta}})^{1/2}>\lambda\norm{\wh\bDelta_S}_{1}
, (
S.36
) implies

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\mathbb{F}}\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{2}\lambda\norm{\wh\bDelta_S}_{1}+t_{2}(\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\mathbb{F}}\widehat{\bm{\Delta}})^{1/2}\leq\frac{5}{2}t_{2}(\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\mathbb{F}}\widehat{\bm{\Delta}})^{1/2},

and therefore
\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\mathbb{F}}\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}\leq\frac{25}{4}t_{2}^{2}
. Since
t_{2}(\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\mathbb{F}}\widehat{\bm{\Delta}})^{1/2}>\lambda\norm{\wh\bDelta_S}_{1}
holds in case (2), we immediately obtain
\norm{\wh\bDelta_S}_{1}\leq\frac{5t_{2}^{2}}{2\lambda}
. To control
\norm{\wh\bDelta_{S^c}}_{1}
, we rely on (
S.36
) again, which is

\frac{1}{2}\lambda\norm{\wh\bDelta_{S^c}}_{1}\leq\frac{3}{2}\lambda\norm{\wh\bDelta_S}_{1}+t_{2}(\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\mathbb{F}}\widehat{\bm{\Delta}})^{1/2}.

This leads to
\norm{\wh\bDelta_{S^c}}_{1}\leq 3\norm{\wh\bDelta_S}_{1}+\frac{5t_{2}^{2}}{\lambda}\leq\frac{25t_{2}^{2}}{2\lambda}
, such that
\|\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}\|_{1}\lesssim t_{2}^{2}/\lambda
. Combining the bounds in these two cases, we derive

\|\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}\|_{1}\lesssim\lambda s_{B}+t_{2}^{2}/\lambda,

where
\lambda
is subject to the constraint that
\lambda\geq 2t_{1}
. To establish a sharp rate of
\|\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}\|_{1}
, we can further minimize
f(\lambda)=\lambda s_{B}+t_{2}^{2}/\lambda
subject to the constraint
\lambda\geq 2t_{1}
. Define
\lambda_{opt}=t_{2}/s_{B}^{1/2}
. When
\lambda_{opt}\geq 2t_{1}
, the minimizer of
f(\lambda)
is
\lambda_{opt}
and the resulting minimal value is
f(\lambda_{opt})=s_{B}^{1/2}t_{2}
. However, when
\lambda_{opt}<2t_{1}
, by the monotonicity of
f(\lambda)
the minimal is given by
f(2t_{1})\asymp t_{1}s_{B}
. Combining these two cases, finally, we obtain the desired rate

\|\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}\|_{1}\lesssim t_{1}s_{B}+s_{B}^{1/2}t_{2}.

With a slight modification of the proof (e.g.,
|I_{1}|\lesssim K_{1}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\|\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}\|_{1}
still holds uniformly over
1\leq k\leq p
), we obtain the same rate for
\|\widehat{\mathbb{B}}_{\cdot k}-\mathbb{B}_{\cdot k}\|_{1}
uniformly over
1\leq k\leq p
. This concludes the proof.

∎

Recall that
\widehat{\bf Z}_{i}={\bf X}_{i}\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})-\widehat{\bm{\mu}}^{j}
,
{\bf Z}_{i}={\bf X}_{i}m({\bf X}_{i})-{\bm{\mu}}
and
\widehat{\mathbb{F}}=\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}\widehat{\bf Z}_{i}^{\otimes 2}

Lemma
S.6

(RE condition for
\widehat{\mathbb{B}}_{k}
)
.

Assume that the same conditions in Theorem
1
hold. Then with probability tending to 1,

\inf_{{\bm{v}}\in\mathcal{C},{\bm{v}}\neq 0}\frac{{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\mathbb{F}}{\bm{v}}}{\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}^{2}}\geq C,

where
\mathcal{C}=\{{\bm{v}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}:\exists S\subseteq\{1,...,p\},|S|=s_{B},\norm{\bv_{S^c}}_{1}\leq\xi\norm{\bv_S}_{1}\}
for some constants
C,\xi>0
.

Proof.

We define
\mathbb{F}=\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}{\bf Z}_{i}^{\otimes 2}
. It holds that

\displaystyle{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\mathbb{F}}{\bm{v}}

\displaystyle={\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\mathbb{F}}-\mathbb{F}){\bm{v}}+{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\{\mathbb{F}-{\hbox{E}}(\mathbb{F})\}{\bm{v}}+{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\hbox{E}}(\mathbb{F}){\bm{v}}

\displaystyle\geq{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\hbox{E}}(\mathbb{F}){\bm{v}}-|{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\mathbb{F}}-\mathbb{F}){\bm{v}}|-|{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\{\mathbb{F}-{\hbox{E}}(\mathbb{F})\}{\bm{v}}|.

In what follows, we will bound the three terms in the last line one by one. Clearly,

\displaystyle{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\hbox{E}}(\mathbb{F}){\bm{v}}\geq C\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}^{2}

(S.37)

uniformly over
{\bm{v}}
, as
{\hbox{E}}(\mathbb{F})
has bounded smallest eigenvalues. For the last term,

|{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\{\mathbb{F}-{\hbox{E}}(\mathbb{F})\}{\bm{v}}|\leq\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2}\|\mathbb{F}-{\hbox{E}}(\mathbb{F})\|_{\max}\leq s_{B}(\xi+1)^{2}\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}^{2}\|\mathbb{F}-{\hbox{E}}(\mathbb{F})\|_{\max}\lesssim s_{B}\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}^{2}K_{1}^{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}},

where the second step holds as
\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}\leq(\xi+1)\|{\bm{v}}_{S}\|_{1}\leq(\xi+1)s_{B}^{1/2}\|{\bm{v}}_{S}\|_{2}\leq(\xi+1)s_{B}^{1/2}\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}
and the last step is obtained by the Hoeffding inequality together with the bound
\|{\bf X}_{i}m({\bf X}_{i})\|_{\infty}\leq CK_{1}
. Under the condition
s_{B}K_{1}^{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}=o(1)
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{{\bm{v}}\in\mathcal{C},{\bm{v}}\neq 0}\frac{|{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\{\mathbb{F}-{\hbox{E}}(\mathbb{F})\}{\bm{v}}|}{\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}^{2}}=o(1).

(S.38)

Now, we focus on the second term
|{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\mathbb{F}}-\mathbb{F}){\bm{v}}|
. To this end, we first note that

\displaystyle|{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\mu}}^{j}-{\bm{\mu}})|

\displaystyle\leq\Big|\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i}\{\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})-m({\bf X}_{i})\}\Big|+\Big|\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}[{\bf X}_{i}m({\bf X}_{i})-{\hbox{E}}\{{\bf X}_{i}m({\bf X}_{i})\}]\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\Big|\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}({\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i})^{2}\Big|^{1/2}\Big|\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}\{\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})-m({\bf X}_{i})\}^{2}\Big|^{1/2}

\displaystyle~~~~~~+\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}\Big\|\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}[{\bf X}_{i}m({\bf X}_{i})-{\hbox{E}}\{{\bf X}_{i}m({\bf X}_{i})\}]\Big\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}K_{1}\left(c_{n}+\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right),

(S.39)

which is implied by the Hoeffding inequality in the last step and
\|\widehat{m}^{-j}-m\|_{2}\lesssim c_{n}
. In addition,

\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}({\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i})^{2}\{\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})-m({\bf X}_{i})\}^{2}\lesssim\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2}K_{1}^{2}c_{n}^{2}.

Combined with (
S.4.2
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}[{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i}\{\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})-m({\bf X}_{i})\}-{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\mu}}^{j}-{\bm{\mu}})]^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}[{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i}\{\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})-m({\bf X}_{i})\}]^{2}+\frac{2}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}\{{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\mu}}^{j}-{\bm{\mu}})\}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2}K_{1}^{2}\left(c_{n}+\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right)^{2}.

(S.40)

An implication of (
S.4.2
) is the following inequality

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}[{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i}\{\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})+m({\bf X}_{i})\}-{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\mu}}^{j}+{\bm{\mu}})]^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}[{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i}\{\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})-m({\bf X}_{i})\}-{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\mu}}^{j}-{\bm{\mu}})]^{2}+\frac{2}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}\{2{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i}m({\bf X}_{i})-2{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\mu}}\}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2}K_{1}^{2}\left(c_{n}+\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right)^{2}+\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2}K_{1}^{2}\lesssim\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2}K_{1}^{2}.

(S.41)

Finally, applying Cauchy–Schwarz inequality we can show that

\displaystyle|{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\mathbb{F}}-\mathbb{F}){\bm{v}}|

\displaystyle=\Big|\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}\Big[\left\{{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i}\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})-{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\mu}}\right\}^{2}-\left\{{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i}m({\bf X}_{i})-{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\mu}}\right\}^{2}\Big]\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}[{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i}\{\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})-m({\bf X}_{i})\}-{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\mu}}-{\bm{\mu}})][{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}X_{i}\{\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})+m({\bf X}_{i})\}-{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\mu}}+{\bm{\mu}})]\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\Big|\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}[{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i}\{\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})-m({\bf X}_{i})\}-{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\mu}}^{j}-{\bm{\mu}})]^{2}\Big|^{1/2}

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\times\Big|\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in D_{j}^{*}}[{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i}\{\widehat{m}^{-j}({\bf X}_{i})+m({\bf X}_{i})\}-{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\widehat{\bm{\mu}}^{j}+{\bm{\mu}})]^{2}\Big|^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}^{2}K_{1}^{2}\left(c_{n}+\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right)\lesssim\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}^{2}s_{B}K_{1}^{2}\left(c_{n}+\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\right),

(S.42)

where we use (
S.4.2
) and (
S.4.2
). Therefore, from (
S.37
), (
S.38
) and (
S.4.2
), we obtain

\inf_{{\bm{v}}\in\mathcal{C},{\bm{v}}\neq 0}\frac{{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}\widehat{\mathbb{F}}{\bm{v}}}{\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}^{2}}\geq C-o(1).

∎

S.5
Some Technical Details

S.5.1
Sparsity assumption on
\mathbb{B}

Here, we first comprehensively illustrate the meaning and the implication of the sparsity assumption on the coefficient matrix
\mathbb{B}
, then we investigate one scenario where this sparsity assumption is satisfied, together with a concrete example.

Meaning and implications of the sparsity of
\mathbb{B}
.

Recall that the matrix
\mathbb{B}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times p}
is defined as the coefficient matrix where
{\bf X}(Y-{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})
is the response and
{\bf X}m({\bf X})-{\bm{\mu}}
is the covariate; i.e.,

{\bf X}(Y-{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})=\mathbb{B}^{\rm T}\{{\bf X}m({\bf X})-{\bm{\mu}}\}+{\bf E}.

For each coordinate
j
, one can write

\displaystyle X_{j}(Y-{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})=\sum_{k=1}^{p}B_{kj}\,\{X_{k}m({\bf X})-\mu_{k}\}+E_{j}.

Thus, the
j
th column of
\mathbb{B}
is sparse; i.e.,
|\{k:B_{kj}\neq 0\}|\ll p
, indicates that, among all the
p
variables
\{X_{k}m({\bf X})-\mu_{k}\}
,
k=1,\ldots,p
, only a small portion of them are truly contributing to the outcome
X_{j}(Y-{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})
.

The matrix
\mathbb{B}
has the closed-form expression

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}=({\hbox{E}}[\{{\bf X}m({\bf X})-{\bm{\mu}}\}^{\otimes 2}])^{-1}{\hbox{E}}\{{\bf X}^{\otimes 2}m({\bf X})(Y-{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})\}.

From this expression, whether the sparsity assumption can be satisfied is driven by a few factors, such as the choice of the function
m({\bf x})
, and the structure of covariate
{\bf X}
.
Below, we first provide general justification for a scenario in which this assumption is naturally satisfied. Then, we present a concrete example.

General justification: when covariate
{\bf X}
has the blockwise independence structure.

We denote the support of function
m
by
S_{m}\subseteq\{1,...,p\}
which is the index set of all the variables present in
m(\cdot)
. Assume that the predictor variables exhibit block
independence with blocks corresponding to the block-diagonal covariance matrix
{\bm{\Sigma}}
, and the maximal block-size is equal to
b_{\max}
. Under this assumption, we firstly know that for
1\leq k\leq p
, if
k\notin S_{m}
,

\|{\hbox{E}}[\{{\bf X}m({\bf X})-{\bm{\mu}}\}X_{k}m({\bf X})]\|_{0}\leq b_{\max},

where we use
X_{k}
to denote the
k
th component of
{\bf X}
. Otherwise,

\|{\hbox{E}}[\{{\bf X}m({\bf X})-{\bm{\mu}}\}X_{k}m({\bf X})]\|_{0}\leq b_{\max}|S_{m}|.

Therefore, we can view the covariance matrix of
{\bf X}m({\bf X})
as a different block-diagonal matrix with the same blocks as in
{\bm{\Sigma}}
for those variables not in working model
m
. Moreover, we can claim that
({\hbox{E}}[\{{\bf X}m({\bf X})-{\bm{\mu}}\}^{\otimes 2}])^{-1}
presents the same block structure as in covariance matrix of
{\bf X}m({\bf X})
.

On the other hand, for
k\notin S_{m}\cup S_{\eta}
, where
\eta({\bf X})=f({\bf X})-{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
,

\|{\hbox{E}}[\{{\bf X}X_{k}m({\bf X})\eta({\bf X})\}]\|_{0}\leq b_{\max},

and the non-zero elements are within the dependence block of
X_{k}
.
Therefore, given that

\mathbb{B}_{\cdot k}=({\hbox{E}}[\{{\bf X}m({\bf X})-{\bm{\mu}}\}^{\otimes 2}])^{-1}{\hbox{E}}[\{{\bf X}X_{k}m({\bf X})\eta({\bf X})\}],

(S.43)

we know
\|\mathbb{B}_{\cdot k}\|_{0}\leq b_{\max}
since
B_{jk}
is nonzero only when
j
is in the corresponding dependence block of
X^{k}
.
For
k\in S_{m}\cup S_{\eta}
,

\|{\hbox{E}}[\{{\bf X}X_{k}m({\bf X})\eta({\bf X})\}]\|_{0}\leq b_{\max}|S_{m}\cup S_{\eta}|,

and by equation (
S.43
) and the block structure of
({\hbox{E}}[\{{\bf X}m({\bf X})-{\bm{\mu}}\}^{\otimes 2}])^{-1}
, we know

\|\mathbb{B}_{\cdot k}\|_{0}\leq b_{\max}|S_{m}\cup S_{\eta}|.

This investigation indicates that, if the supports of working model
m(\cdot)
and
\eta(\cdot)
, and the nonlinear part in
f(\cdot)
are sparse, the blockwise independence structure of
{\bf X}
assumption is sufficient to guarantee the sparsity of
\mathbb{B}
.

The following example even indicates, in some special cases, only the blockwise independence structure of
{\bf X}
can guarantee the sparsity of
\mathbb{B}
.

Concrete example.

Let
p=2M
with
M\geq 2
and partition the features into independent bivariate blocks

\displaystyle{\bf X}=\left(\hbox to32.76pt{\vbox to33.38pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 16.37775pt\lower-28.98819pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-8.36945pt}{-24.61351pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{68}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right)^{\rm T},\qquad G_{r}\sim N\left({\bf 0},\begin{bmatrix}1&\rho\\
\rho&1\end{bmatrix}\right),

where
G_{r}
independent across
r
, and
|\rho|<1
.
Now, consider

\displaystyle f({\bf X})={\bm{\theta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}+\sum_{r=1}^{M}\gamma_{r}\left\{(X_{2r-1})^{2}-1\right\}.

By some simple algebra, one can compute that

{\bm{\theta}}^{*}={\bm{\theta}}
, hence
\eta({\bf X})=\sum_{r=1}^{M}\gamma_{r}\left\{(X_{2r-1})^{2}-1\right\}
.
In the following derivations, we take
m({\bf X}):=\sum_{r=1}^{M}\left\{(X_{2r-1})^{2}-1\right\}
, adopt the simpler notation that
{\bf Z}_{m}({\bf X}):={\bf X}m({\bf X})
, then it is clear that
{\bm{\mu}}={\hbox{E}}\{{\bf X}m({\bf X})\}={\bf 0}
.
By block independence and symmetry, both
{\hbox{E}}({\bf Z}_{m}{\bf Z}_{m}^{\rm T})
and
{\hbox{E}}\{{\bf X}{\bf X}^{\rm T}m({\bf X})\eta({\bf X})\}
are block‑diagonal with identical
2\times 2
blocks across
r
:

\displaystyle{\hbox{E}}\left({\bf Z}_{m}{\bf Z}_{m}^{\rm T}\right)=\mathrm{diag}\left({\bf A},\ldots,{\bf A}\right),\qquad{\hbox{E}}\left\{{\bf X}{\bf X}^{\rm T}m({\bf X})\eta({\bf X})\right\}=\mathrm{diag}\left({\bf C}_{1},\ldots,{\bf C}_{M}\right),

where,

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}=\begin{bmatrix}2M+8&\rho(2M+8)\\
\rho(2M+8)&2M+8\rho^{2}\end{bmatrix},\qquad\mathbf{C}_{r}=\begin{bmatrix}2G+8\gamma_{r}&\rho(2G+8\gamma_{r})\\
\rho(2G+8\gamma_{r})&2G+8\rho^{2}\gamma_{r}\end{bmatrix},

where
G:=\sum_{r=1}^{M}\gamma_{r}
.
Therefore, each block of
\mathbb{B}
is

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}_{r}\;=\;\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{C}_{r}\;=\;\begin{bmatrix}\dfrac{G+4\gamma_{r}}{M+4}&\dfrac{4\rho\,(M\gamma_{r}-G)}{M(M+4)}\\[10.0pt]
0&\dfrac{G}{M}\end{bmatrix},\qquad r=1,\ldots,M.

Hence
\mathbb{B}=\mathrm{diag}(\mathbf{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{B}_{M})
is block‑diagonal, hence sparse.
An interesting special case is when
\gamma_{r}\equiv 1
, then
\mathbf{B}_{r}={\bf I}_{2}
for all
r
, then
\mathbb{B}
becomes the identity matrix
{\bf I}_{p}
.

S.5.2
The formal proof that the condition
0<\psi<2
is necessary and sufficient

Recall that

\displaystyle{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}={\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i1}^{\otimes 2})-\frac{N(2\psi-\psi^{2})}{n+N}\,\left\{{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}{\bf T}_{i1}^{\rm T})\right\}^{\rm T}\left\{{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}^{\otimes 2})\right\}^{-1}{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}{\bf T}_{i1}^{\rm T}),\qquad\mathbb{K}={\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i1}^{\otimes 2}).

Define

\displaystyle c_{\psi}:=\frac{N(2\psi-\psi^{2})}{n+N},\quad{\bf A}:={\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}{\bf T}_{i1}^{\rm T}),\quad{\bf C}:={\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}^{\otimes 2})(\succ{\bf 0}).

Then
{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}=\mathbb{K}-c_{\psi}{\bf A}^{\rm T}{\bf C}^{-1}{\bf A}
.
For any
{\bm{v}}\neq{\bf 0}
,

\displaystyle{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}={\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}\mathbb{K}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}-c_{\psi}\,{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bf A}^{\rm T}{\bf C}^{-1}{\bf A}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}={\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}\mathbb{K}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}-c_{\psi}\,\big\|\,{\bf C}^{-1/2}{\bf A}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}\,\big\|_{2}^{2}.

If
2\psi-\psi^{2}>0
(equivalently
0<\psi<2
), then
c_{\psi}>0
and

\displaystyle{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}\leq{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}\mathbb{K}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}},

with equality if and only if
{\bf A}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}={\bf 0}
. In Remark
2
, we assume
{\bf A}
has full rank (equivalently
{\bf A}^{\rm T}{\bf C}^{-1}{\bf A}\succ{\bf 0}
), hence the inequality is strict for all
{\bm{v}}\neq{\bf 0}
:

\displaystyle{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}<{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}\mathbb{K}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}.

On the other hand, if
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}<{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}\mathbb{K}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}
, then it follows that
c_{\psi}\,\big\|\,{\bf C}^{-1/2}{\bf A}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}\,\big\|_{2}^{2}>0
. Because
{\bf A}
is of full rank which implies that
\|{\bf C}^{-1/2}{\bf A}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}\|_{2}^{2}>0
for any
{\bm{v}}\neq{\bf 0}
, it comes that
c_{\psi}>0
which implies
0<\psi<2
.
This completes the proof.

S.5.3
Efficiency gain compared to the supervised estimator

We are able to see the efficiency gain by computing the relative efficiency between the supervised and the semi-supervised estimators.
Note that the proposed semi-supervised estimator admits the asymptotic variance
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}
,
while the supervised estimator has the asymptotic variance
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}\mathbb{K}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}
,
with

\displaystyle{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}=\mathbb{K}-(1-\rho)(2\psi-\psi^{2})\mathbb{M},\qquad\mathbb{K}={\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i1}^{\otimes 2}),

and
\mathbb{M}:=\{{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}{\bf T}_{i1}^{\rm T})\}^{\rm T}\{{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}^{\otimes 2})\}^{-1}{\hbox{E}}({\bf T}_{i2}{\bf T}_{i1}^{\rm T})
.
Thus, the relative efficiency equals

\displaystyle\frac{{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\psi}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}}{{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}\mathbb{K}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}}=1-(1-\rho)(2\psi-\psi^{2})\frac{{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}\mathbb{M}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}}{{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}\mathbb{K}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}}.

Clearly, if we have more unlabeled data,
\rho
gets smaller, then we have a smaller relative efficiency that indicates a greater efficiency gain.
Also, it is clear that the optimal choice of
\psi
is 1.
The term
{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}\mathbb{M}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}/{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}{\bm{\Omega}}\mathbb{K}{\bm{\Omega}}{\bm{v}}
is always bounded between 0 and 1, since
\mathbb{K}-\mathbb{M}
is positive semi-definite.
In the special situation that
{\bf T}_{i1}
is a linear function of
{\bf T}_{i2}
,
\mathbb{K}
=
\mathbb{M}
and this terms becomes 1,
where
{\bf T}_{i1}={\bf X}_{i}(Y_{i}-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*})
and
{\bf T}_{i2}={\bf X}_{i}m({\bf X}_{i})-{\bm{\mu}}
.

S.5.4
Choice of functional class
{\mathcal{G}}
for estimating
m({\bf x})

The main assumption regarding the estimator
\widehat{m}({\bf x})
in Assumption
1
pertains to the deterministic sequence
c_{n}
where

\|\widehat{m}^{-j}-m\|_{2}=O_{p}(c_{n})
.
It is required that
s_{B}K_{1}^{2}\Big(c_{n}+\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\Big)=o(1)
.

In practice, we recommend that users of our method choose the function class
{\mathcal{G}}
from commonly used ones, such as linear functions, additive functions, interaction models
(
63
)
, single-index models
(
38
,
58
,
19
)
, or multi-index models
(
57
)
.

If the function class
{\mathcal{G}}
is chosen to be sparse linear, Theorem 7.20 in
50
showed that
c_{n}\asymp\sqrt{s\log p/n}
.
Then the requirement becomes to
s_{B}K_{1}^{2}\sqrt{s\log p/n}=o(1)
.
If the function class
{\mathcal{G}}
is chosen to be sparse additive, from some oracle inequalities for sparse additive estimator
(
27
)
as well as classical nonparametric rate
(
46
)
, it implies that
c_{n}
can be chosen as

\displaystyle\sqrt{s}\,n^{-\alpha/(2\alpha+1)}+\sqrt{s\log p/n},

where it is assumed that each active univariate component has smoothness parameter
\alpha>0
.
For more complex choices of
{\mathcal{G}}
, relevant theoretical results can be found in the respective literature, such as those for interaction models
(
63
)
, single-index models
(
38
,
58
,
19
)
, and multi-index models
(
57
)
.

S.5.5
Comparison with related work in Section
3

When the dimension
p
is fixed and small,
3
and
13
investigated how to incorporate the unlabeled data to improve the estimation efficiency for regression coefficients in a working linear regression. In addition to the technical challenges arise from the high dimensionality (e.g., regularization and one-step update), a key difference from the previous works is that our dependable semi-supervised approach leads to a more efficient estimator for any linear combination of
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
. In the following, we briefly summarize their methodologies and explain the differences.

To improve the estimation efficiency for
\theta_{j}^{*}
,
3
considered the following adjusted linear regression, for any
j\in[p]

\widetilde{Y}_{ij}=\theta^{*}_{j}+{\bm{\phi}}_{j}^{\rm T}{\bf U}_{ij}+\widetilde{\delta}_{ij},

where
\widetilde{Y}_{ij}=Y_{i}\widetilde{X}_{ij}
,
{\bf U}_{ij}=(U_{ij1},...,U_{ijp})^{\rm T}
with
U_{ijk}=X_{ik}\widetilde{X}_{ij}
for
k\neq j
and
U_{ijj}=X_{ij}\widetilde{X}_{ij}-1
and
\widetilde{\delta}_{ij}
is a mean 0 random variable. We use the notation
\widetilde{X}_{ij}=(X_{ij}-{\bm{\gamma}}_{j}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i,-j})/{\hbox{E}}\{(X_{ij}-{\bm{\gamma}}_{j}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i,-j})^{2}\}
where
{\bm{\gamma}}_{j}
is the estimand for the nodewise lasso (
S.9
). One interesting property of the adjusted linear regression is that the parameter of interest
\theta_{j}^{*}
becomes the intercept parameter, because
{\hbox{E}}({\bf U}_{ij})=0
and
\theta_{j}^{*}={\hbox{E}}(Y_{i}\widetilde{X}_{ij})={\hbox{E}}(\widetilde{Y}_{ij})
by the definition of
\widetilde{Y}_{ij}
. Thus, when
p
is fixed and small,
\theta^{*}_{j}
can be estimated by
\widehat{\theta}_{j}^{A}
the LSE from the adjusted linear regression, where the unlabeled data can help the estimation of
{\bm{\gamma}}_{j}
and
{\hbox{E}}\{(X_{ij}-{\bm{\gamma}}_{j}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i,-j})^{2}\}
. Thanks to the orthogonality of
\widetilde{\delta}_{ij}
and
{\bf U}_{ij}
, the asymptotic variance of
\widehat{\theta}_{j}^{A}
is shown to be no greater than
\widehat{\theta}_{j}^{LSE}
, the
j
th component of the standard LSE
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{LSE}=(\mathbb{X}^{\rm T}\mathbb{X})^{-1}\mathbb{X}^{\rm T}{\bf Y}
; see their Theorem 2. As a result, if the parameter of interest is any component of
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
, their estimator provides the dependable semi-supervised inference.

However, since the adjusted linear regression is estimated for each
j\in[p]
separately, their procedure does not guarantee the orthogonality of
\widetilde{\delta}_{ij}
and
{\bf U}_{ij^{\prime}}
for any
j^{\prime}\neq j
when the true regression function
f({\bf X})
is nonlinear. Therefore, the linear combination of their estimators such as
\widehat{\theta}_{j}^{A}+\widehat{\theta}_{j^{\prime}}^{A}
may not be more efficient than the standard LSE
\widehat{\theta}_{j}^{LSE}+\widehat{\theta}_{j^{\prime}}^{LSE}
. Unlike their approach, our estimator is constructed based on the geometric interpretation of estimating functions. The projection theory from estimating functions motivates us to consider the working regression model (
5
), which is different from the adjusted linear regression in
3
.

13
proposed a class of Efficient and Adaptive Semi-Supervised Estimators (EASE) which exploit the unlabeled data based on a semi-non-parametric smoothing and refitting
estimate of a target imputation function
\mu({\bf X})
. They mainly focused on the context that
N
is much larger than
n
and
p
is fixed. With an estimated imputation function
\widehat{\mu}({\bf X})
, they derived an initial semi-supervised estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{r}
through the estimating equation

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=n+1}^{n+N}{\bf X}_{i}\left\{\widehat{\mu}({\bf X}_{i})-{\bf X}_{i}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}\right\}={\bf 0}.

(S.44)

The estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{r}
attains the semi-parametric efficiency bound when the imputation is sufficient (i.e., the imputation function equals the conditional mean function
\mu({\bf X})=f({\bf X})
) or the conditional mean function
f({\bf X})
is linear
f({\bf X})={\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
. As seen in Remark
S.2
, these properties also hold for our efficient semi-supervised estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
in (
4
). To ensure the improved efficiency of EASE, they considered a further step of calibration which searched an optimal linear combination of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{r}
and the LSE
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{LSE}
. Their adaptive estimator is defined as
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{E}=\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{LSE}+{\bm{\Delta}}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{r}-\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{LSE})
, where
{\bm{\Delta}}
is a diagonal matrix that minimizes the asymptotic variance of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{E}_{j}
for each
j\in[p]
. When
{\bm{\Delta}}
is consistently estimated,
\widehat{\theta}^{E}_{j}
is always no less efficient than the LSE no matter whether the imputation is sufficient or
f({\bf X})
is linear. However, by the construction of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{E}
, the efficiency improvement is not guaranteed if a linear combination of
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
is considered.

S.5.6
The difference between our framework and variable/feature importance

The setting we adopt in this paper is the so-called assumption-lean framework.
This framework is suitable when one in interested in some simple but interpretable parameter of interest, such as the association between a certain phenotype and SNPs in the genome-wide association study.
This framework does not have to be confined in the linear working model.
In fact, in Section 4, we extend the methodology to a more general M-estimation framework, and
we have clarified that the key ideas carry over to this broader setting without substantial difficulty.

Variable importance is a relevant, but distinct, concept.
In recent years, owing to its interpretability and generality, variable importance has attracted significant interest in the literature, particularly when estimated with flexible machine learning methods; see, e.g.
54
,
55
,
48
, as well as applications in survival analysis
(
56
)
and causal inference
(
21
)
.
Many variable importance measures exist, such as the one based on the Shapley value
(
49
)
.
Below, we briefly review one of such measures.
Using the notation we use in our paper, decompose the covariate
{\bf X}
as
{\bf X}=({\bf X}_{1}^{\rm T},{\bf X}_{2}^{\rm T})^{\rm T}
and denote
f({\bf X})={\hbox{E}}(Y\mid{\bf X})
,
h({\bf X})={\hbox{E}}(Y\mid{\bf X}_{2})
, then the importance of covariate
{\bf X}_{1}
can be defined as

\displaystyle\psi={\hbox{E}}\left[\{m({\bf X})-h({\bf X})\}^{2}\right].

By construction, the parameter
\psi
quantifies the improvement in predictive performance achieved by incorporating
{\bf X}_{1}
, relative to solely using
{\bf X}_{2}
.

While variable importance measures are inherently model-agnostic, their primary objective is to quantify the predictive value of specific features.
In contrast, our goal in this paper is to estimate and conduct inference on a generally defined parameter (e.g., an association measure derived via a linear working model or a general M-estimator) within an assumption-lean framework.
Therefore, the two objectives are fundamentally distinct.

Figure S.1
:
Simulation results for
Model 1
with
p=500
: absolute difference between the empirical 95% coverage probability and the nominal level 0.95.
In all panels, rows represent different parameters, columns represent different
N/n
ratios, and each panel plots the trend over the sample size
n
.

We consider the following additive model for
Y
, that

Y=0.5X_{1}^{2}+0.8X_{3}^{3}-(X_{4}-2)^{2}+2(X_{5}+1)^{2}+2X_{6}+\epsilon
,
where
\epsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.
Similar to Section
5
, we first generate a
p
-dimensional multivariate normal random vector
{\bf U}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,{\bm{\Sigma}})
with
\Sigma_{jk}=0.3^{|j-k|}
. We set the covariate
{\bf X}=(X_{1},...,X_{p})^{\rm T}
to be
X_{1}=|U_{1}|
and
X_{j}=U_{j}~\mathrm{for}~1<j\leq p
. The reason we take
X_{1}=|U_{1}|
is that this transformation implies
{\hbox{E}}(X_{1}^{k}X_{j})=0
for
j\neq 1
but the parameter
\theta^{*}_{1}
for centered
X_{1}
is nonzero.
To calculate the corresponding regression parameter
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
under the working linear model, we first center
Y
and
X_{1}
so that their means are 0. By Proposition 4 in
7
, we know that the support of
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
is
S=\{1,3,4,5,6\}
and
\theta_{j}^{*}
for any
j\in S
is given by the
L_{2}({\mathbb{P}})
projection in the sub-model only with the variable
X_{j}
(e.g,
\theta_{3}^{*}=\arg\min{\hbox{E}}(0.8X_{3}^{2}-\theta_{3}X_{3})^{2}
).
After some calculation, we obtain
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}=(1.1,0,2.4,4,4,2,0,...,0)^{\rm T}
, which is sparse.

With sample size
n\in\{100,300\}
, the ratio
N/n\in\{1,4,8\}
and the dimension
p\in\{200,500\}
, we compare the performance of the four methods:
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{1}
(D-Lasso1, that only uses labeled data with sample size
n
),
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{2}
(D-Lasso2), both defined in (
18
), the straightforward debiased estimator D-SSL
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}
defined in (
4
), and the proposed dependable semi-supervised estimator S-SSL
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{d}_{S,\psi=1}
defined in (
9
).

Based on 100 simulation replicates, we report the empirical bias (Bias), standard deviation (SD), root mean squared error (RMSE), the half length of 95% confidence interval (len/2) and the coverage probability (CVP) for each of the single parameters
\theta_{1}
,
\theta_{3}
,
\theta_{4}
,
\theta_{5}
and
\theta_{6}
, for
p=200
and
p=500
, in Table
S.2
and Table
S.3
, respectively.
In Table
S.4
, we also report the computation time (in seconds) of one simulation replication of these four methods.

It is seen that the coverage rates of all the methods are very close to the desired level
0.95
, especially when the sample size
n=300
.
In the majority of the scenarios we consider, the D-SSL method produces the shortest CIs among all four methods, and the CIs from the S-SSL method are always shorter than those from D-Lasso1 and D-Lasso2.
As expected, the CIs from both D-SSL and S-SSL become shorter as the size of unlabeled data
N
increases.
Finally, we note that the length of CIs from D-Lasso1 and D-Lasso2 is very similar, which shows that the way of estimating
{\bm{\Omega}}
has little effect on debiased lasso estimators.

In addition, in Table
S.5
, we compare the performance of the proposed method S-SSL with different methods of estimating the conditional mean function
f(\cdot)
. Other than the method in
23
(huang), we also implement the random forest method (randomforest) and the neural network method (nnet).
Under the setting we consider, all three methods perform very similar and it is hard tell which one might be better.
In Table
S.6
, we compare the performance of the proposed dependable semi-supervised estimator S-SSL with different criteria of choosing the tuning parameters
(\lambda_{\Omega},\lambda_{B})
in estimating
({\bm{\Omega}},\mathbb{B})
.
Here,
\lambda_{\min}
refers to the value of the tuning parameter
\lambda
that yields the minimum mean cross-validated error, and
\lambda_{1se}
refers to the largest (most regularized, or ”simplest”) value of the tuning parameter
\lambda
such that the cross-validation error is within one standard error of the minimum error achieved by
\lambda_{\min}
.
Across the four scenarios we compare, their performances are very similar which indicates that the proposed estimator is not sensitive to the tuning parameter selection.
Further, in Table
S.7
, we assess the performance of the proposed method S-SSL for different linear combinations of the parameter of interest:
-\theta_{1}+2\theta_{6}
(case 1),
-\theta_{1}+\theta_{3}+2\theta_{6}
(case 2),
-\theta_{1}+\theta_{3}+\theta_{5}+\theta_{6}
(case 3), and
-\theta_{1}+\theta_{3}-\theta_{4}+\theta_{5}
(case 4).
In addition, in Table
S.8
, we assess the performance of the proposed method S-SSL for different estimands where the value
\|{\bm{v}}\|_{1}/\|{\bm{v}}\|_{2}
ranges from 1 to
\sqrt{5}
.
